### Question 149
A triage nurse in the emergency department is evaluating a 16-year-old client who presents with restlessness, prominent bulging eyes (exophthalmos), and a heart rate of 118 beats per minute. The client's parent states, "She is always hot and eats constantly but keeps losing weight." Based on these observations, which nursing intervention should the nurse implement while the client waits to be seen by the provider?

A) Provide a warm blanket and a hot beverage to help the client relax.
B) Place the client in a cool, quiet room to decrease environmental stimulation.
C) Encourage the client to perform vigorous exercises to burn off excess energy.
D) Restrict the client's oral fluid intake to prevent fluid volume overload.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
The client is exhibiting classic signs of hyperthyroidism (Graves' disease), including tachycardia, heat intolerance, exophthalmos, and weight loss despite an increased appetite. Because these clients are in a hypermetabolic state, they are highly sensitive to external stimuli and prone to hyperthermia. Placing the client in a cool, quiet environment helps reduce metabolic demands and promote rest. Giving warm blankets (A) or encouraging vigorous exercise (C) would worsen their heat intolerance and tachycardia. Fluid restriction (D) is not indicated, as these clients often have increased insensible fluid loss from sweating.
</details>

---

### Question 150
A nurse in the intensive care unit is caring for a 55-year-old client who is 12 hours post-operative following the transsphenoidal removal of a pituitary tumor. The nurse notes that the client has produced 1,500 mL of pale, clear urine over the last hour and is complaining of extreme thirst. Which assessment should the nurse anticipate performing next?

A) Check the client's capillary blood glucose level.
B) Measure the client's abdominal girth for distention.
C) Check the urine specific gravity.
D) Auscultate the client's lungs for crackles.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
Following pituitary surgery, clients are at a high risk for developing central diabetes insipidus (DI) due to a sudden deficiency in antidiuretic hormone (ADH). Massive amounts of dilute polyuria and extreme thirst are hallmark signs of DI. The nurse should anticipate checking the urine specific gravity, which will be abnormally low (dilute) in a client with DI. While blood glucose (A) can cause polyuria, the surgical history points directly to DI. Abdominal distention (B) and crackles (D) are not directly related to the massive fluid loss seen in this specific clinical picture; in fact, DI causes dehydration, not fluid overload.
</details>
### Question 151

A school nurse is called to the playground where a 9-year-old child with type 1 diabetes is found pale, sweating profusely, and appearing confused. What is the nurse's immediate priority action?

A) Administer a rapid-acting insulin injection.
B) Give the child 15 grams of a fast-acting carbohydrate.
C) Encourage the child to drink 8 ounces of plain water.
D) Check the child's urine for ketones.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) Give the child 15 grams of a fast-acting carbohydrate.

**Rationale:** The child is exhibiting classic signs of hypoglycemia (pallor, diaphoresis, and altered mental status). According to the "Rule of 15" for conscious hypoglycemic patients, the nurse should immediately provide 15 grams of a fast-acting carbohydrate (such as juice or glucose tablets) to rapidly raise the blood glucose level. Administering insulin (Option A) would worsen the hypoglycemia and could be fatal. Drinking plain water (Option C) does not treat the low blood sugar. Checking for ketones (Option D) is indicated for hyperglycemia, not hypoglycemia.
</details>

---

### Question 152

A nurse in an outpatient clinic is performing an admission assessment on a 68-year-old female client newly diagnosed with hypothyroidism. Which statement by the client is most consistent with this diagnosis during the nurse's assessment?

A) "I feel cold all the time, even when it's warm in the house."
B) "I have been losing weight despite eating a lot more."
C) "I have to go to the bathroom to have a bowel movement five times a day."
D) "My heart feels like it is constantly racing."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) "I feel cold all the time, even when it's warm in the house."

**Rationale:** Hypothyroidism causes a generalized decrease in the body's metabolic rate. Classic, easy-to-recognize symptoms include cold intolerance, weight gain, constipation, fatigue, and bradycardia. Therefore, feeling constantly cold is an expected assessment finding. Weight loss despite increased appetite (Option B), frequent bowel movements/diarrhea (Option C), and a racing heart/tachycardia (Option D) are classic signs of hyperthyroidism, not hypothyroidism.
</details>

---

### Question 153

A 45-year-old female is admitted to the medical-surgical unit with Cushing's syndrome. While performing the morning physical assessment, which finding should the nurse anticipate observing?

A) A noticeable decrease in facial and body hair.
B) Dark, bronze pigmentation of the oral mucosa.
C) Prominent fat deposition at the back of the neck.
D) Unexplained and profound weight loss over the past month.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) Prominent fat deposition at the back of the neck.

**Rationale:** Cushing's syndrome is caused by an excess of corticosteroids (glucocorticoids). Common clinical manifestations observed by the nurse include central obesity, a "moon face," and a fat pad at the back of the neck, often referred to as a "buffalo hump." Decreased body hair is incorrect; hirsutism (increased hair growth) is more common in Cushing's (Option A). Bronze pigmentation (Option B) and profound weight loss (Option D) are classic signs of Addison's disease (adrenal insufficiency), which is the opposite of Cushing's syndrome.
</details>

---

### Question 154

A 28-year-old client is recovering in the intensive care unit following the surgical removal of a pituitary tumor. The nurse notes the client has produced 1,500 mL of very pale urine in the last hour and is complaining of extreme thirst. Which action should the nurse take first?

A) Restrict the client's oral fluid intake immediately.
B) Administer a prescribed loop diuretic.
C) Prepare to administer an oral antidiabetic medication.
D) Notify the healthcare provider of the findings.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) Notify the healthcare provider of the findings.

**Rationale:** Polyuria (excessive urination) and polydipsia (excessive thirst) following pituitary surgery are hallmark signs of Diabetes Insipidus (DI), a condition caused by a deficiency of antidiuretic hormone (ADH). The nurse must notify the provider immediately so that appropriate treatment (such as desmopressin and fluid replacement) can be prescribed. Restricting fluids (Option A) is dangerous and can lead to severe dehydration and hypovolemic shock. Administering a diuretic (Option B) would worsen the fluid loss. Oral antidiabetics (Option C) are used for Diabetes Mellitus, which is a blood glucose disorder, not Diabetes Insipidus.
</details>

---

### Question 155

A nurse is caring for a 32-year-old male client in a telemetry unit who has a history of severe Graves' disease (hyperthyroidism). During the assessment, the nurse observes that the client's eyes appear unusually prominent and bulging. Which nursing intervention is most appropriate to include in the care plan for this specific observation?

A) Apply warm compresses to the client's eyes every 2 hours.
B) Administer artificial tears to keep the client's eyes moist.
C) Keep the head of the bed completely flat at all times.
D) Instruct the client to rub their eyes frequently to stimulate tears.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) Administer artificial tears to keep the client's eyes moist.

**Rationale:** Exophthalmos (bulging eyes) is a common manifestation of Graves' disease. Because the eyes bulge, the eyelids may not close completely, leading to dry, irritated corneas and a risk for corneal ulceration. The nurse should administer artificial tears to maintain moisture. Warm compresses (Option A) are not standard treatment and do not provide continuous moisture. The head of the bed should be elevated, not flat, to help reduce periorbital edema (Option C). The client should be instructed NOT to rub their eyes (Option D), as this can cause further irritation or injury to the exposed cornea.
</details>
### Question 156
A nurse is performing an admission assessment on a 72-year-old client in a skilled nursing facility who has a new diagnosis of hypothyroidism. Based on this diagnosis, which clinical manifestation should the nurse expect to observe during the assessment?

A) Frequent reports of feeling uncomfortably hot
B) Unexplained weight loss despite an increased appetite
C) Complaints of chronic fatigue and constipation
D) A resting heart rate of 110 beats per minute

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) Complaints of chronic fatigue and constipation

**Rationale:** Hypothyroidism results in a decreased metabolic rate. As a result, nurses will commonly observe symptoms such as chronic fatigue, constipation, weight gain, cold intolerance, and bradycardia (slow heart rate). Feeling hot, weight loss, and tachycardia (rapid heart rate) are classic signs of hyperthyroidism, not hypothyroidism.

</details>

### Question 157
A nurse in an urgent care center is caring for a 10-year-old child with type 1 diabetes mellitus. The child appears visibly pale, is sweating profusely, and reports feeling very shaky after missing lunch. What is the nurse's priority action?

A) Provide the child with 4 ounces of fruit juice.
B) Administer the child's scheduled dose of regular insulin.
C) Encourage the child to drink a large glass of plain water.
D) Instruct the child to lie down and rest quietly for 30 minutes.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) Provide the child with 4 ounces of fruit juice.

**Rationale:** The child is exhibiting classic signs of hypoglycemia (pallor, diaphoresis, and tremors) likely caused by skipping a meal. The nurse's immediate priority is to provide a fast-acting, easily absorbed carbohydrate, such as 4 ounces of fruit juice, to quickly raise the blood glucose level. Administering insulin would fatally lower the blood sugar further. Drinking water or simply resting will not correct the underlying hypoglycemia.

</details>

### Question 158
A nurse on a medical-surgical unit is developing a care plan for a 45-year-old male client with Cushing's syndrome. When providing daily hygiene and skin care, which intervention is most important for the nurse to implement?

A) Massage bony prominences vigorously to stimulate circulation.
B) Apply heating pads to areas of striae to reduce skin discoloration.
C) Restrict daily fluid intake to prevent severe peripheral edema.
D) Use a lift sheet when repositioning the client in bed.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) Use a lift sheet when repositioning the client in bed.

**Rationale:** Clients with Cushing's syndrome have an excess of corticosteroids, which leads to thin, fragile skin that is highly susceptible to bruising, tearing, and breakdown. Using a lift sheet prevents shearing and friction forces that could easily tear the client's skin. Massaging bony prominences can cause tissue damage and is contraindicated. Heating pads pose a major burn risk to fragile skin, and fluid restriction is not a direct skin-care intervention.

</details>

### Question 159
A nurse in the emergency department is monitoring a 16-year-old client who sustained a head injury and is suspected of developing diabetes insipidus (DI). Which finding should the nurse report to the healthcare provider immediately?

A) A blood glucose level of 95 mg/dL
B) A urine output of 1,200 mL over the last hour
C) A blood pressure reading of 120/80 mmHg
D) A client report of a mild, localized headache

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) A urine output of 1,200 mL over the last hour

**Rationale:** Diabetes insipidus (DI) occurs due to a deficiency of antidiuretic hormone (ADH), often following head trauma or brain surgery. It causes massive diuresis (polyuria), which can quickly lead to severe dehydration and hypovolemic shock. A urine output of 1,200 mL in one hour is dangerously high and requires immediate medical intervention. The blood glucose and blood pressure readings are within normal limits, and a mild headache is expected following a head injury.

</details>

---

### Question 160
A nurse is providing discharge instructions to a 28-year-old female client newly diagnosed with hyperthyroidism (Graves' disease). The client asks what lifestyle modifications she should make at home. Which recommendation should the nurse include?

A) Consume a very low-calorie diet to manage unexpected weight gain.
B) Apply extra blankets to the bed at night to prevent episodes of shivering.
C) Take frequent rest breaks in a cool, quiet environment.
D) Increase intake of foods high in dietary fiber to resolve sluggish bowels.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) Take frequent rest breaks in a cool, quiet environment.

**Rationale:** Hyperthyroidism causes a hypermetabolic state, leading to symptoms such as heat intolerance, nervous exhaustion, tachycardia, and weight loss. Providing a cool, quiet environment helps reduce metabolic demands, promotes rest, and decreases irritability. These clients typically require a high-calorie diet due to weight loss, experience heat intolerance (so extra blankets would be uncomfortable), and often have frequent bowel movements or diarrhea rather than sluggish bowels.

</details>
### Question 161
A nurse in an outpatient clinic is providing discharge instructions to a 68-year-old patient who has been newly prescribed levothyroxine for primary hypothyroidism. Which statement by the patient indicates a correct understanding of how to take this medication?

A) "I should take this medication right before I go to sleep at night."
B) "I will take this pill every morning on an empty stomach."
C) "I should take this medicine with my breakfast to prevent stomach upset."
D) "I will only take this medication on days when I am feeling tired or cold."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
Levothyroxine should be taken in the morning on an empty stomach, typically 30 to 60 minutes before breakfast, to ensure optimal absorption. Taking it with food or at night can decrease its effectiveness. It is a daily maintenance medication, not a PRN (as-needed) drug.
</details>

### Question 162
A school nurse is called to the gymnasium to evaluate a 10-year-old student with Type 1 diabetes mellitus. The student is sweating profusely, trembling, and appears confused after running laps. A capillary blood glucose check shows a reading of 52 mg/dL. Which action should the nurse take first?

A) Administer the student's scheduled dose of regular insulin.
B) Send the student to the cafeteria to eat a full hot meal.
C) Provide the student with 4 ounces of regular apple juice.
D) Encourage the student to drink a large bottle of water and rest.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
The student is experiencing symptomatic hypoglycemia (blood glucose < 70 mg/dL). The immediate nursing action is to provide a fast-acting, simple carbohydrate, such as 4 ounces of regular fruit juice, to quickly raise the blood glucose level. Giving insulin would worsen the hypoglycemia, and a full meal takes too long to digest for acute management. 
</details>

### Question 163
A nurse on a medical-surgical unit is caring for a 45-year-old patient with Graves' disease. During the morning assessment, the nurse notes notable exophthalmos (bulging eyes) and the patient reports having dry, irritated eyes. Which nursing intervention is most appropriate to include in the patient's daily care plan?

A) Keep the head of the patient's bed completely flat at all times.
B) Instruct the patient to avoid wearing sunglasses when going outside.
C) Apply warm, dry heating pads directly over the patient's closed eyes.
D) Administer artificial tears as prescribed to keep the eyes moist.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br>
Patients with exophthalmos due to Graves' disease (hyperthyroidism) are at high risk for corneal dryness, irritation, and injury because the eyelids may not close completely. Administering artificial tears helps maintain moisture. The head of the bed should be elevated (not flat) to reduce periorbital edema, and sunglasses should be worn outdoors to protect the eyes from wind and glare.
</details>

### Question 164
A 28-year-old patient is recovering in the surgical intensive care unit following the removal of a pituitary tumor. The nurse notes that the patient has produced 2,500 mL of very pale, odorless urine over the last two hours and is complaining of extreme, unquenchable thirst. Based on these observations, which complication should the nurse suspect the patient is developing?

A) Diabetes insipidus
B) Syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone (SIADH)
C) Diabetic ketoacidosis
D) Acute kidney injury

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
Diabetes insipidus (DI) is a common complication after pituitary surgery due to a deficiency in antidiuretic hormone (ADH). It presents with sudden, massive polyuria (large amounts of dilute urine) and polydipsia (extreme thirst). SIADH presents with decreased urine output. 
</details>

### Question 165
A nurse in a long-term care facility is performing a routine skin assessment on a 72-year-old resident who has a documented history of Cushing's syndrome. Based on the patient's diagnosis of prolonged cortisol excess, which physical finding should the nurse expect to observe?

A) Unintentional weight loss and severe muscle hypertrophy
B) Thin, fragile skin with frequent ecchymosis (bruising)
C) A consistently low resting heart rate and low blood pressure
D) Thick, hardened, and darkly pigmented skin on the arms

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
Cushing's syndrome is caused by an excess of corticosteroids (cortisol). Common clinical manifestations include thin and fragile skin that bruises easily, truncal obesity, muscle wasting (not hypertrophy), and hypertension (not hypotension). Recognizing these skin changes is essential for preventing skin tears and injury in older adults.
</details>
### Question 166
A nurse in an outpatient clinic is providing discharge instructions to a 45-year-old female client newly prescribed levothyroxine for hypothyroidism. Which statement by the client indicates that the teaching was successful?

A. "I will take this medication with my dinner every evening."
B. "I should take this pill first thing in the morning on an empty stomach."
C. "I can stop taking this medication once my energy levels return to normal."
D. "I will take it alongside my calcium supplement to boost absorption."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
Levothyroxine should be taken in the morning on an empty stomach, at least 30 to 60 minutes before the first meal of the day, to maximize its absorption. It should not be taken with meals (Option A) or with calcium/iron supplements (Option D), as these can significantly decrease the absorption of the medication. Hypothyroidism is a chronic condition, and the medication is typically taken for life, not just until symptoms resolve (Option C).
</details>

### Question 167
A school nurse is called to the cafeteria to evaluate a 12-year-old boy with Type 1 Diabetes Mellitus. The student is shaking, sweating, and appears confused. The nurse uses a glucometer and notes a blood glucose reading of 58 mg/dL. What is the nurse's priority action?

A. Administer 4 ounces of regular, non-diet fruit juice.
B. Administer a scheduled dose of rapid-acting insulin.
C. Encourage the student to drink a can of diet cola.
D. Have the child lie down and rest for 15 minutes before rechecking the blood sugar.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
The child is exhibiting signs of symptomatic hypoglycemia (blood glucose < 70 mg/dL). For a conscious patient who can swallow, the nurse should administer 15 grams of fast-acting carbohydrates, such as 4 ounces of regular fruit juice or regular soda. Diet soda does not contain sugar and will not correct the hypoglycemia (Option C). Giving insulin would further lower the blood sugar and be extremely dangerous (Option B). Resting without intervening first ignores the immediate risk of a severe hypoglycemic event, including seizures or a coma (Option D).
</details>

### Question 168
A nurse on a medical-surgical unit is completing an admission assessment on a 58-year-old male client diagnosed with Cushing's syndrome. Based on this diagnosis, which physical manifestation should the nurse expect to observe?

A. Unexplained weight loss and hyperactive bowel sounds.
B. Hypotension and dark, bronze pigmentation of the skin.
C. Purple striae on the abdomen and a rounded "moon" face.
D. Intolerance to cold and dry, flaky skin.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
Cushing's syndrome is caused by an excess of corticosteroids (specifically glucocorticoids). Classic clinical manifestations include a rounded "moon" face, central obesity, a "buffalo hump" on the upper back, and purple striae (stretch marks) on the abdomen. Weight loss is associated with hyperthyroidism or Addison's disease (Option A). Hypotension and bronze skin are classic signs of Addison's disease, which is a deficiency of corticosteroids (Option B). Cold intolerance and dry skin are typical of hypothyroidism (Option D).
</details>

### Question 169
The nurse is caring for a 28-year-old female in the neurosurgical intensive care unit following the removal of a pituitary tumor. The nurse suspects the client has developed Diabetes Insipidus (DI). Which finding in the client's output record supports this complication?

A. Concentrated, dark urine with a high specific gravity.
B. Elevated blood glucose levels requiring insulin drips.
C. Significant fluid retention and bounding peripheral pulses.
D. Copious amounts of pale, dilute urine output.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br>
Diabetes Insipidus (DI) occurs due to a deficiency of antidiuretic hormone (ADH), often following pituitary surgery or head trauma. Without ADH, the kidneys cannot reabsorb water, leading to massive diuresis of very pale, dilute urine and a low specific gravity. Dark, concentrated urine with a high specific gravity indicates SIADH or dehydration (Option A). DI is unrelated to insulin or blood glucose regulation, despite the word "diabetes" in its name (Option B). Fluid retention and bounding pulses are signs of fluid volume overload, whereas DI causes extreme fluid volume deficit (Option C).
</details>

---

### Question 170
A nurse is planning care for a 35-year-old female client admitted to the general ward with an acute exacerbation of hyperthyroidism (Graves' disease). Which nursing intervention should be included in the client's care plan to promote comfort?

A. Provide extra blankets to prevent shivering.
B. Offer three large, heavy meals a day to prevent weight loss.
C. Keep the room temperature cool and minimize environmental stimuli.
D. Encourage participation in high-energy aerobic exercises to burn energy.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
Clients with hyperthyroidism have a hypermetabolic state, leading to heat intolerance, diaphoresis, tachycardia, and increased anxiety. Keeping the room cool and providing a calm, low-stimulus environment helps manage these symptoms. Providing extra blankets would worsen heat intolerance (Option A). Because of their hypermetabolic state, these patients need frequent, small, high-calorie meals (up to 6 times a day) rather than three large, heavy meals which can be difficult to consume at once (Option B). High-energy exercise is contraindicated during an acute exacerbation as it further increases metabolic demands and cardiovascular strain (Option D).
</details>
### Question 171
A nurse in an outpatient clinic is taking the health history of a 55-year-old female client recently diagnosed with hypothyroidism. Which client statement should the nurse expect to document as an expected finding?

A. "I feel hot all the time, even when the air conditioning is on."
B. "I have been losing weight recently despite eating constantly."
C. "I need to wear a sweater in the house because I am always freezing."
D. "I have been having frequent episodes of diarrhea over the last month."

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** C

**Rationale:** Hypothyroidism results in a decreased metabolic rate, which commonly causes cold intolerance. The nurse should expect the client to report feeling cold even in warm environments. Options A, B, and D are classic manifestations of *hyperthyroidism*, where the metabolic rate is abnormally elevated, leading to heat intolerance, weight loss, and increased gastrointestinal motility.
</details>

### Question 172
A school nurse is called to the playground to assist a 10-year-old male student with type 1 diabetes mellitus. The nurse observes that the student is pale, profusely sweating, and reports feeling shaky. Which action should the nurse take first?

A. Provide the student with 4 ounces of regular fruit juice.
B. Administer the student's scheduled dose of regular insulin.
C. Encourage the student to drink a large glass of water.
D. Instruct the student to lie flat on the grass and rest for 15 minutes.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** A

**Rationale:** The student is exhibiting classic, easily recognizable signs of hypoglycemia (pallor, diaphoresis, and tremors). The nurse's immediate priority is to raise the student's blood glucose level by providing a fast-acting, simple carbohydrate, such as 4 ounces of regular fruit juice. Administering insulin (Option B) would fatally lower the blood sugar further. Drinking water (Option C) or just resting (Option D) will not correct the dangerous deficit in circulating glucose.
</details>

### Question 173
A nurse on a medical-surgical unit is caring for a 32-year-old female client admitted with an exacerbation of Graves' disease. The nurse notes that the client has prominent, bulging eyes (exophthalmos). Which nursing intervention is most appropriate to include in the client's care plan?

A. Apply warm, moist compresses to the client's eyes every two hours.
B. Tape the client's eyelids completely shut during daytime hours.
C. Position the client flat in bed to promote ocular drainage.
D. Administer prescribed artificial tears to keep the eyes moist.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** D

**Rationale:** Exophthalmos, a common finding in Graves' disease (hyperthyroidism), prevents the eyelids from closing completely, leaving the exposed eyes prone to severe dryness and corneal ulceration. The nurse should frequently administer artificial tears to maintain moisture. Warm compresses (Option A) are not standard and can cause irritation; cool compresses are preferred. Taping eyelids shut during the day (Option B) is inappropriate and impairs vision. The head of the bed should be elevated, not flat (Option C), to help reduce periorbital edema.
</details>

### Question 174
A nurse at a long-term care facility is performing a morning physical assessment on a 68-year-old male resident who has been taking high-dose corticosteroids for several months. Which physical finding should the nurse recognize as a classic manifestation of medication-induced Cushing's syndrome?

A. Dark, bronze-colored pigmentation on the hands and face.
B. A rounded "moon" face and a fatty pad on the upper back.
C. Significant unintentional weight loss and muscle spasms.
D. Bulging eyes and an enlarged, palpable goiter.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** B

**Rationale:** Cushing's syndrome involves an excess of glucocorticoids (like cortisol), often from prolonged corticosteroid therapy. The nurse should easily recognize the hallmark signs of altered fat distribution, which include a rounded "moon" face, a "buffalo hump" (fat pad on the upper back), and truncal obesity. Bronze pigmentation (Option A) is associated with Addison's disease. Weight loss (Option C) is incorrect, as Cushing's causes weight gain. Bulging eyes and a goiter (Option D) are signs of hyperthyroidism.
</details>

### Question 175
An emergency department nurse is completing a triage assessment on a 45-year-old female client with a known history of Addison's disease. The client reports severe dizziness upon standing. When asking about recent dietary habits, which specific food craving should the nurse recognize as an expected compensatory finding for this condition?

A. Heavily salted snacks like pretzels and potato chips.
B. Dairy products such as milk and thick cheeses.
C. Large amounts of refined sugars and sweet pastries.
D. Ice chips and raw, uncooked starches.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** A

**Rationale:** Addison's disease involves a deficiency in mineralocorticoids (aldosterone), which leads to significant sodium loss and potassium retention in the kidneys. The nurse should recognize that clients with this condition often crave salty foods as a physiological attempt to compensate for hyponatremia. Craving dairy (Option B) or sugars (Option C) does not relate to the specific electrolyte imbalances of Addison's. Craving ice or starches (Option D) is known as pica, typically associated with iron-deficiency anemia.
</details>
### Question 176
A 14-year-old adolescent with Type 1 diabetes presents to the school clinic after gym class. The nurse observes that the student is shaking, sweating profusely, and appears confused. A capillary blood glucose reading shows 58 mg/dL. What is the most appropriate initial action by the nurse?

A. Provide 4 ounces of apple juice to the student  
B. Administer a scheduled dose of regular insulin  
C. Give the student a high-protein snack like a piece of cheese  
D. Wait 15 minutes and recheck the capillary blood glucose  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Details</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> The student is experiencing symptomatic hypoglycemia (blood glucose less than 70 mg/dL). The "Rule of 15" dictates that the nurse should immediately provide 15 grams of fast-acting carbohydrates, such as 4 ounces of fruit juice, to rapidly raise the blood glucose level. Giving insulin would worsen the hypoglycemia. A high-protein snack without carbohydrates will not raise blood glucose quickly enough for an acute episode. Waiting 15 minutes without intervention is unsafe and could lead to loss of consciousness or seizures. 
</details>

---

### Question 177
A nurse in an outpatient clinic is providing discharge teaching to a 62-year-old female who was newly prescribed levothyroxine for hypothyroidism. Which statement by the patient indicates that the teaching was successful?

A. "I will take this medicine with my dinner every evening."  
B. "I should take this medication with an antacid if it upsets my stomach."  
C. "I will take this pill in the morning on an empty stomach."  
D. "I need to take this pill right before I go to bed at night."  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Details</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Levothyroxine should be taken first thing in the morning on an empty stomach, at least 30 to 60 minutes before breakfast. This ensures optimal and consistent absorption of the medication. Taking it with food, antacids, or at night can significantly decrease its absorption and effectiveness.
</details>

### Question 178
A 45-year-old male patient with Graves' disease is admitted to the medical-surgical unit. The nurse notes that the patient has pronounced exophthalmos (bulging eyes) and the patient complains that his eyes feel dry, gritty, and irritated. Which action should the nurse take to alleviate the patient's discomfort?

A. Advise the patient to avoid wearing sunglasses when going outdoors.  
B. Instruct the patient to strictly limit fluid intake to reduce orbital swelling.  
C. Apply warm, dry compresses to the patient's eyes every few hours.  
D. Administer artificial tear drops as prescribed to maintain moisture.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Details</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Exophthalmos caused by hyperthyroidism prevents the eyelids from closing completely, leading to dry, irritated corneas and an increased risk of corneal abrasions. Providing artificial tears helps keep the eyes moist and lubricated. Patients should wear sunglasses outdoors to protect from wind and glare. Fluid restriction is not indicated for exophthalmos, and dry compresses would not provide the necessary moisture and could further irritate the eyes.
</details>

### Question 179
A nurse is coordinating care for a 78-year-old female resident in a long-term care facility who has Cushing's syndrome. The patient requires assistance with morning hygiene and mobility. Which specific action should the nurse instruct the unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) to perform during morning care?

A. Assess the patient's fragile skin for any new areas of bruising.  
B. Use a lift sheet when repositioning the patient in bed.  
C. Evaluate the patient's lower legs for the presence of pitting edema.  
D. Apply a prescribed medicated corticosteroid cream to dry skin patches.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Details</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Patients with Cushing's syndrome have extremely fragile, thin skin that is prone to tearing and bruising. Using a lift sheet or transfer board prevents shearing forces and protects the patient's skin integrity during repositioning. Assessing skin, evaluating for edema, and applying prescribed medicated creams are nursing responsibilities that cannot be delegated to a UAP. 
</details>

### Question 180
A home health nurse is observing the daily self-care routine of a 68-year-old male with a long history of Type 2 diabetes and peripheral neuropathy. Which action performed by the patient requires immediate intervention and correction by the nurse?

A. The patient applies a heavy layer of moisturizing lotion between his toes.  
B. The patient inspects the bottoms of his feet using a handheld mirror.  
C. The patient puts on well-fitting, closed-toe leather walking shoes.  
D. The patient uses a nail clipper to cut his toenails straight across.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Details</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Patients with diabetes should not apply lotion between their toes. Moisture accumulation in this tight space creates a favorable environment for fungal infections and skin maceration, which can easily lead to non-healing foot ulcers. Inspecting the feet with a mirror, wearing closed-toe shoes, and cutting nails straight across are all correct and highly encouraged diabetic foot care practices.
</details>
### Question 181
A school nurse is called to the playground for a 9-year-old child with type 1 diabetes who is trembling, sweating profusely, and complaining of dizziness. The child is conscious, alert, and able to swallow without difficulty. What is the nurse's immediate action?

A. Administer regular insulin subcutaneously to the child.  
B. Give the child 4 ounces of apple juice to drink.  
C. Encourage the child to drink a large glass of plain water.  
D. Have the child rest quietly in a dark room for 15 minutes before re-evaluating.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
The child is exhibiting classic, easy-to-recognize signs of hypoglycemia (sweating, trembling, dizziness). Because the child is conscious and can safely swallow, the standard immediate nursing action is to provide a fast-acting carbohydrate, such as 4 ounces of juice, to rapidly raise the blood glucose level. Administering insulin would fatally lower the blood sugar further.
</details>

### Question 182
A nurse in an outpatient clinic is providing discharge instructions to a 45-year-old female recently diagnosed with hypothyroidism. The healthcare provider has prescribed daily levothyroxine. Which statement by the nurse describes the correct administration of this medication?

A. "Take the medication each morning on an empty stomach with a full glass of water."  
B. "Take the medication right before bedtime accompanied by a small protein snack."  
C. "Crush the pill and mix it with a dairy-based food like yogurt to prevent stomach upset."  
D. "Take the medication with your largest meal of the day to ensure maximum absorption."  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
Levothyroxine should be taken in the morning on an empty stomach, typically 30 to 60 minutes before breakfast. This ensures maximum and consistent absorption of the medication. Taking it with food (especially dairy/calcium) can significantly impair its absorption, and taking it at bedtime can cause insomnia.
</details>

### Question 183
A nurse in a long-term care facility is admitting a 72-year-old male resident who has Cushing's syndrome resulting from prolonged corticosteroid therapy for rheumatoid arthritis. During the initial physical assessment, which finding should the nurse anticipate observing?

A. Significant, unexplained weight loss over the past several months.  
B. Deep, hyperpigmented, bronze-colored skin on the hands and face.  
C. Intolerance to cold temperatures and complaints of chronic constipation.  
D. Thin, fragile skin with visible purple striae on the abdomen.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br>
Cushing's syndrome is caused by an excess of cortisol. Classic physical findings include thin and fragile skin, purple striae (stretch marks) on the abdomen, a "moon face," and truncal obesity. Weight loss is associated with hyperthyroidism, bronze skin is associated with Addison's disease, and cold intolerance is a hallmark of hypothyroidism.
</details>

---

### Question 184
A 28-year-old female with Graves' disease is admitted to a medical-surgical unit experiencing tachycardia, excessive sweating, and severe anxiety. When planning daily care and the environment for this patient, which intervention should the nurse implement?

A. Keep the room temperature warmer than usual to prevent shivering.  
B. Encourage the patient to participate in high-intensity aerobic exercises to burn off energy.  
C. Provide a cool room environment and offer frequent, high-calorie snacks.  
D. Restrict the patient's oral fluid intake to 1 liter per day to prevent edema.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
Graves' disease is a form of hyperthyroidism characterized by a hypermetabolic state. Patients typically experience heat intolerance and rapid weight loss. Therefore, the nurse should ensure a cool, comfortable environment to reduce sweating and discomfort, and provide frequent, high-calorie meals and snacks to meet the patient's increased metabolic demands. 
</details>

### Question 185
A nurse in a pediatric intensive care unit is caring for a 16-year-old who recently sustained a severe closed head injury. The adolescent suddenly begins producing large amounts of very pale urine and complains of intense, unquenchable thirst. Which assessment should the nurse perform next to evaluate for suspected diabetes insipidus?

A. Check the patient's blood glucose level using a point-of-care glucometer.  
B. Measure the patient's urine specific gravity.  
C. Assess the patient's deep tendon reflexes in the lower extremities.  
D. Auscultate the patient's bowel sounds in all four abdominal quadrants.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
Traumatic head injuries can disrupt the pituitary gland, leading to a decrease in antidiuretic hormone (ADH) production and resulting in diabetes insipidus (DI). DI is characterized by massive polyuria and extreme thirst. The nurse should check the urine specific gravity, which will be abnormally low (highly dilute) due to the body's inability to concentrate urine without ADH.
</details>
### Question 186
A nurse in an outpatient clinic is assessing a 45-year-old female client recently diagnosed with hypothyroidism. Which clinical manifestation should the nurse expect to observe during the physical assessment?

A) Reports of frequent diarrhea and unintentional weight loss.
B) A resting heart rate of 110 beats per minute.
C) The client wearing a thick winter sweater despite a warm room temperature.
D) Bulging of the eyes and a flushed, moist face.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
Hypothyroidism causes a significantly decreased metabolic rate. The nurse should expect to observe signs of a slowed metabolism, such as cold intolerance (e.g., wearing heavy clothing in a warm room), weight gain, fatigue, bradycardia, and constipation. Options A, B, and D are classic signs of hyperthyroidism, not hypothyroidism.
</details>

### Question 187
A school nurse is called to the playground to evaluate a 10-year-old boy with Type 1 Diabetes Mellitus. The nurse observes that the child is sweating profusely, trembling, and appears confused after running laps. Which action should the nurse take first?

A) Administer 4 ounces of regular fruit juice.
B) Inject rapid-acting insulin subcutaneously.
C) Encourage the child to drink 8 ounces of plain water.
D) Call the parents to immediately pick the child up from school.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
The child is exhibiting classic signs of hypoglycemia (sweating, trembling, and confusion) brought on by physical exertion. The nurse's immediate priority is to raise the blood glucose level by administering 15 grams of a fast-acting simple carbohydrate, such as 4 ounces of regular fruit juice. Administering insulin would worsen the hypoglycemia, and water will not raise blood glucose levels.
</details>

### Question 188
A nurse on a medical-surgical unit is caring for a 28-year-old male client admitted with an acute exacerbation of Graves' disease. While taking the client's vital signs and performing a morning assessment, which finding should the nurse anticipate?

A) Lethargy and a notably decreased respiratory rate.
B) A consistently subnormal body temperature.
C) Complaints of chronic constipation and abdominal bloating.
D) A resting heart rate of 118 beats per minute.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br>
Graves' disease is an autoimmune disorder that causes hyperthyroidism, leading to a hypermetabolic state. The nurse should anticipate signs of an overactive metabolism, such as tachycardia (a heart rate over 100 bpm), heat intolerance, weight loss, diarrhea, and restlessness. Options A, B, and C are symptoms associated with a slowed metabolism, typical of hypothyroidism.
</details>

### Question 189
A nurse in a long-term care facility is reviewing the care plan for a 68-year-old female resident with iatrogenic Cushing's syndrome due to long-term corticosteroid therapy. Which physical characteristic should the nurse expect to document during the routine skin and body assessment?

A) Generalized hyperpigmentation of the gums and oral mucosa.
B) A rounded facial appearance and a fat pad on the upper back.
C) Profound weight loss and decreased muscle tone in the abdomen.
D) Decreased body hair and thickened, pale skin.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
Cushing's syndrome is characterized by an excess of glucocorticoids (cortisol). The nurse should expect to see characteristic fat redistribution, commonly referred to as a "moon face" (a rounded facial appearance) and a "buffalo hump" (a prominent fat pad on the upper back), along with truncal obesity and thin extremities. Option A is characteristic of Addison's disease.
</details>

### Question 190
A nurse in the intensive care unit is monitoring a 35-year-old male client who is recovering from transsphenoidal surgery to remove a pituitary tumor. The nurse notes the client's urinary output was 1,500 mL over the last two hours. Which action is the priority for the nurse to perform next?

A) Restrict the client's oral and intravenous fluid intake immediately.
B) Administer a prescribed loop diuretic to clear excess fluid.
C) Check the urine specific gravity and assess the client's blood pressure.
D) Palpate the client's abdomen to assess for bladder distention.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
Following pituitary surgery, clients are at a high risk for developing Diabetes Insipidus (DI) due to a deficiency in antidiuretic hormone (ADH). Massive polyuria is a hallmark sign. The nurse must immediately check the urine specific gravity (which will be abnormally low/dilute) and assess blood pressure, as the massive fluid loss can quickly lead to hypovolemic shock. Restricting fluids or giving diuretics would severely harm a patient actively losing fluid.
</details>
### Question 191
A clinic nurse is educating a 45-year-old female client recently diagnosed with hypothyroidism who has been prescribed levothyroxine. Which statement by the client indicates an understanding of the medication instructions?

A) "I will take this medication with my dinner every evening."  
B) "I need to take this pill on an empty stomach first thing in the morning."  
C) "I can stop taking the medication once my energy levels return to normal."  
D) "I will take this pill with a glass of milk to prevent stomach upset."  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
Levothyroxine must be taken on an empty stomach, typically 30 to 60 minutes before breakfast, to ensure optimal absorption. Taking it with food or calcium-rich products like milk (Option A and D) impairs absorption. Hypothyroidism is a lifelong condition, and the medication should not be stopped once symptoms improve (Option C).
</details>

### Question 192
A school nurse is called to the playground for an 8-year-old boy with a history of Type 1 diabetes mellitus. The child is sweating profusely, trembling, and appears confused. What is the nurse's immediate priority action?

A) Administer a rapid-acting insulin dose subcutaneously.  
B) Encourage the child to drink a large glass of water.  
C) Provide the child with 4 ounces of regular apple juice.  
D) Send the child back to the classroom to rest for 15 minutes.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
The child is displaying classic clinical signs of hypoglycemia (sweating, tremors, confusion). The immediate, easy-level intervention for a conscious, hypoglycemic client is to provide a fast-acting simple carbohydrate, such as 4 ounces of juice, to rapidly raise blood glucose levels. Giving insulin (Option A) would worsen the hypoglycemia and could be fatal. Water and rest (Options B and D) do not address the low blood sugar.
</details>

### Question 193
A nurse on a medical-surgical unit is caring for a 65-year-old male client admitted with Cushing's syndrome. When assisting the client with morning hygiene, which intervention should the nurse prioritize to prevent complications?

A) Handle the client's skin gently and use a moisturizing lotion.  
B) Encourage the client to use a hard-bristled toothbrush to prevent gingivitis.  
C) Apply tight-fitting compression stockings to prevent joint swelling.  
D) Vigorously massage the client's calves to promote circulation.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
Clients with Cushing's syndrome have an excess of cortisol, which leads to thin, fragile skin that is highly susceptible to tearing, bruising, and infection. The nurse must handle the skin gently and keep it moisturized to maintain skin integrity. Hard-bristled toothbrushes (Option B) can cause bleeding. Vigorous massages and tight stockings (Options C and D) can cause severe tissue damage and bruising in these clients.
</details>

### Question 194
An emergency department nurse is triaging a 28-year-old female client with a known history of Addison's disease who reports extreme fatigue, nausea, and recent weight loss. Which physical assessment finding should the nurse anticipate observing in this client?

A) A prominent fatty pad on the back of the neck  
B) Bulging of the eyes and a fixed stare  
C) Purple striae across the abdomen  
D) Bronze-colored hyperpigmentation of the skin  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br>
Addison's disease involves primary adrenal insufficiency, which triggers the pituitary gland to increase ACTH production. This overproduction stimulates melanocytes, resulting in the classic expected finding of bronze hyperpigmentation of the skin, especially in sun-exposed areas and skin folds. A fatty pad and purple striae (Options A and C) are signs of Cushing's syndrome, while bulging eyes (Option B) are associated with Graves' disease (hyperthyroidism).
</details>

### Question 195
A nurse in an outpatient clinic is obtaining vital signs and assessing a 35-year-old female client with untreated hyperthyroidism. Which clinical manifestation should the nurse expect to find during the assessment?

A) A core body temperature of 96.8°F (36°C)  
B) A resting heart rate of 115 beats per minute  
C) Complaints of severe constipation  
D) Noticeable weight gain over the last month  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
Hyperthyroidism causes a hypermetabolic state in the body. The nurse should expect signs of an accelerated metabolism, such as tachycardia (a resting heart rate over 100 bpm), heat intolerance, frequent bowel movements/diarrhea, and weight loss. Low body temperature, constipation, and weight gain (Options A, C, and D) are classic signs of a slowed metabolism, which occurs in hypothyroidism.
</details>
### Question 196
A nurse is gathering admission data at a primary care clinic from a 42-year-old client who was recently diagnosed with hypothyroidism. Which clinical finding should the nurse expect to observe during the assessment?

A. Exaggerated reflexes and visible tremors
B. Reports of feeling constantly cold despite wearing a sweater
C. Unintentional weight loss over the past month
D. A resting heart rate of 110 beats per minute

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B. Reports of feeling constantly cold despite wearing a sweater

**Rationale:** Hypothyroidism causes a decrease in the body's overall metabolic rate. An easy-to-spot, classic observation by the nurse is the client's intolerance to cold. Other common expected findings include bradycardia (not tachycardia), weight gain (not weight loss), and sluggish reflexes (not exaggerated reflexes or tremors). 
</details>

### Question 197
A nurse is caring for a 10-year-old client on a pediatric unit with type 1 diabetes mellitus. Suddenly, the client becomes pale, diaphoretic, and irritable. The client's morning routine insulin was administered three hours ago. What is the nurse's immediate action?

A. Encourage the client to drink a large glass of plain water.
B. Administer a supplemental dose of regular insulin.
C. Provide 4 ounces of apple juice to the client.
D. Ask the client to lie down and rest for an hour before re-evaluating.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C. Provide 4 ounces of apple juice to the client.

**Rationale:** Pallor, diaphoresis (sweating), and irritability in a client with diabetes who recently received insulin are classic signs of hypoglycemia. The nurse's immediate action must be to provide a fast-acting, simple carbohydrate, such as 4 ounces of fruit juice, to quickly raise the blood glucose level. Giving insulin would worsen the hypoglycemia, and resting or drinking plain water delays crucial treatment.
</details>

### Question 198
A nurse is planning care for a 30-year-old client admitted to the medical-surgical unit with an acute exacerbation of Graves' disease. While observing the client, the nurse notes severe diaphoresis and restlessness. Which nursing action is most appropriate to include in the plan of care?

A. Keep the client's room temperature cool and limit stimulating visitors.
B. Provide extra thermal blankets to prevent sudden shivering.
C. Encourage daily participation in high-energy group activities.
D. Offer hot, caffeinated beverages to soothe the throat.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A. Keep the client's room temperature cool and limit stimulating visitors.

**Rationale:** Graves' disease (hyperthyroidism) results in a hypermetabolic state, causing heat intolerance, diaphoresis, tachycardia, and severe anxiety or restlessness. The nurse should maintain a calm, cool environment to promote comfort and decrease metabolic demand. Caffeinated beverages, high-energy activities, and extra blankets would all exacerbate the client's symptoms.
</details>

### Question 199
A nurse is monitoring a 50-year-old client in the post-anesthesia care unit (PACU) recovering from the surgical removal of a pituitary tumor. Over the last hour, the nurse observes a sudden, massive urine output of 800 mL of clear, water-like urine. Which action should the nurse take first?

A. Restrict the client's oral and intravenous fluid intake immediately.
B. Administer a prescribed loop diuretic to help clear the excess surgical fluids.
C. Document the output as a normal, expected postoperative finding.
D. Assess the client's blood pressure and notify the healthcare provider.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D. Assess the client's blood pressure and notify the healthcare provider.

**Rationale:** A sudden onset of massive, dilute polyuria following pituitary surgery is a primary indicator of Diabetes Insipidus (DI), caused by a lack of antidiuretic hormone (ADH). This massive fluid loss can quickly lead to severe dehydration and hypovolemic shock. The nurse must assess hemodynamic status (blood pressure) and notify the provider for interventions (such as fluid replacement and desmopressin). Restricting fluids or giving a diuretic would be dangerous.
</details>

---

### Question 200
A nurse in a rehabilitation facility is assisting a 68-year-old client who has Cushing's syndrome with morning hygiene. During the bed bath, the nurse observes that the client's skin is extremely thin and fragile with multiple areas of ecchymosis (bruising). Which intervention should the nurse implement?

A. Vigorously rub the skin dry with a coarse towel to promote circulation.
B. Use a lift sheet to reposition the client and apply moisturizing lotion.
C. Apply warm heating pads to the bruised areas to accelerate tissue healing.
D. Wash the client with an alcohol-based cleanser to prevent skin infections.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B. Use a lift sheet to reposition the client and apply moisturizing lotion.

**Rationale:** Cushing's syndrome involves an excess of corticosteroids, which leads to protein wasting, resulting in extremely thin, fragile skin that is highly prone to bruising and tearing. The nurse must implement strict skin-protection measures, including using a lift sheet (draw sheet) to prevent shearing forces during repositioning, and keeping the skin moisturized. Vigorously rubbing the skin or using harsh alcohol-based cleansers will cause skin breakdown.
</details>
### Question 201
A 65-year-old client in an outpatient clinic is prescribed daily levothyroxine for primary hypothyroidism. The nurse is observing the client's understanding of the new medication schedule. Which statement by the client indicates proper administration?

A) "I will take my pill every morning with a large glass of milk to protect my stomach."
B) "I will take this medication immediately before bedtime with a small snack."
C) "I will take my pill first thing in the morning on an empty stomach with water."
D) "I will take this medication only on the days when I am feeling extremely fatigued."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
Levothyroxine is a synthetic thyroid hormone used to treat hypothyroidism. Its absorption is easily affected by food, especially calcium-rich foods, iron supplements, and antacids. Therefore, the nurse should ensure the client takes it on an empty stomach, typically 30 to 60 minutes before breakfast, with a full glass of water. It must be taken daily, not as needed.
</details>

---

### Question 202
A nurse is caring for a 12-year-old client with type 1 diabetes mellitus on a pediatric unit. The client suddenly reports feeling shaky, sweaty, and confused. A point-of-care capillary blood glucose reading is 52 mg/dL. What is the nurse's immediate action?

A) Administer 4 ounces of apple juice orally.
B) Inject a prescribed dose of regular insulin subcutaneously.
C) Encourage the client to eat a high-fat peanut butter bar.
D) Document the finding and reassess the glucose in one hour.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
The client is experiencing symptomatic hypoglycemia. Following the "rule of 15," the immediate nursing action for a conscious patient who can swallow is to provide 15 grams of a fast-acting, simple carbohydrate, such as 4 ounces of fruit juice or regular soda. Regular insulin would worsen the hypoglycemia. High-fat foods delay the absorption of sugar and are not appropriate for immediate treatment. 
</details>

---

### Question 203
A 34-year-old client admitted to the medical-surgical unit has severe Graves' disease with prominent exophthalmos. During morning rounds, which nursing action is most appropriate to include in this client's care plan to prevent complications?

A) Keep the client's room brightly lit during daylight hours.
B) Tape the client's eyelids tightly closed at all times.
C) Encourage the client to lie completely flat in bed while resting.
D) Administer prescribed artificial tears frequently throughout the day.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br>
Exophthalmos (bulging of the eyes) is a common complication of Graves' disease. The bulging prevents the eyelids from closing properly, which can lead to severe corneal drying, irritation, and potential ulceration. Administering artificial tears frequently keeps the corneas moist. Elevating the head of the bed (not lying flat) helps reduce periorbital edema, and dark glasses or dimming the lights is preferred for photophobia. 
</details>

---

### Question 204
A home health nurse visits a 45-year-old client who has been taking daily hydrocortisone therapy for Addison's disease. The client states, "I feel much better now and I'm tired of taking pills, so I stopped taking my medication yesterday." Which response by the nurse is the priority?

A) "You should switch to taking it every other day instead to taper off."
B) "Stopping this medication suddenly can cause a life-threatening crisis."
C) "It is okay to stop as long as your blood pressure remains normal."
D) "Let's wait a week and see if your extreme fatigue and dizziness return."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
Clients with Addison's disease have adrenal insufficiency and rely on exogenous corticosteroids to survive. Abruptly stopping corticosteroids can precipitate an Addisonian crisis, which is a life-threatening emergency characterized by severe hypotension, vascular collapse, and shock. The nurse must immediately educate the client on the dangers of abruptly discontinuing the medication.
</details>

---

### Question 205
A 28-year-old client is recovering in the intensive care unit following surgical removal of a pituitary tumor. The nurse notes the client has voided 3,000 mL of pale, clear urine over the last two hours and is complaining of extreme, unquenchable thirst. Which action should the nurse take first?

A) Restrict the client's oral fluid intake immediately to prevent fluid overload.
B) Prepare to administer a prescribed intravenous bolus of regular insulin.
C) Check the client's urine specific gravity and notify the healthcare provider.
D) Reassure the client that large amounts of urine are normal after anesthesia.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
Massive polyuria and polydipsia following pituitary surgery are classic clinical signs of Diabetes Insipidus (DI), which occurs due to a lack of antidiuretic hormone (ADH). The nurse should check the urine specific gravity (which will be abnormally low, typically <1.005) and notify the provider so that appropriate treatment, such as IV fluids and desmopressin (DDAVP), can be initiated. Restricting fluids in a patient with DI will rapidly lead to severe dehydration and hypernatremia.
</details>
### Question 206
A school nurse is evaluating a 10-year-old child with Type 1 diabetes who reports feeling "shaky and dizzy" right after playing at recess. The nurse observes that the child's skin is pale and diaphoretic. What is the nurse's priority action?

A) Administer a prescribed dose of rapid-acting insulin.  
B) Provide 4 ounces of apple juice for the child to drink.  
C) Have the child lie down in a dark room to rest.  
D) Call the child's parents to take them home immediately.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
The child is exhibiting classic signs of hypoglycemia (shakiness, dizziness, pallor, and diaphoresis), which frequently occurs after physical activity in clients with Type 1 diabetes. The nurse's immediate action should be to provide a fast-acting carbohydrate, such as 4 ounces of apple juice, to raise the blood glucose level. Administering insulin (Option A) would dangerously worsen the hypoglycemia. Resting (Option C) or calling the parents (Option D) delays essential and immediate treatment, which could lead to loss of consciousness or seizures.
</details>

---

### Question 207
A nurse in a community health clinic is assessing a 68-year-old client who was recently diagnosed with primary hypothyroidism. Based on this diagnosis, which clinical manifestation should the nurse anticipate observing during the assessment?

A) Recent, unexplained weight loss  
B) Chronic diarrhea and abdominal cramping  
C) Intolerance to cold environments  
D) A resting heart rate of 110 beats per minute  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
Hypothyroidism causes a generalized decrease in the body's metabolic rate. As a result, clients typically experience cold intolerance, weight gain, fatigue, bradycardia, and constipation. Unexplained weight loss (Option A), chronic diarrhea (Option B), and tachycardia (Option D) are classic signs of a hypermetabolic state, which is seen in hyperthyroidism, not hypothyroidism.
</details>

---

### Question 208
During an admission assessment on a medical-surgical unit, the nurse examines a 45-year-old male client with a history of long-term corticosteroid use for an autoimmune condition. Which physical observation strongly suggests to the nurse that the client has developed Cushing's syndrome?

A) Extreme generalized muscle hypertrophy  
B) Hyperpigmentation of the palmar creases  
C) Bulging eyes and a visibly enlarged goiter  
D) A rounded "moon" face and truncal obesity  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br>
Cushing's syndrome results from prolonged exposure to excess corticosteroids (hypercortisolism). Easy-to-recognize, classic physical signs include a rounded "moon" face, central (truncal) obesity, a "buffalo hump" on the upper back, and thin extremities. Muscle wasting, rather than hypertrophy (Option A), occurs in Cushing's. Hyperpigmentation (Option B) is a classic sign of Addison's disease (adrenal insufficiency). Bulging eyes (exophthalmos) and a goiter (Option C) are clinical signs of Graves' disease (hyperthyroidism).
</details>

---

### Question 209
A nurse is caring for a 28-year-old client admitted with an acute exacerbation of hyperthyroidism (Graves' disease). When planning the client's room assignment and environmental controls, which action by the nurse is the most appropriate?

A) Keeping the room temperature cool and providing light blankets.  
B) Placing the client in a room directly next to the bustling nurses' station.  
C) Restricting the client's oral fluid intake to 1,000 mL daily.  
D) Providing exclusively high-fiber, low-calorie meals and snacks.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
Clients with hyperthyroidism experience a hypermetabolic state resulting in severe heat intolerance and diaphoresis. Keeping the room cool maximizes the client's comfort. Because these clients are easily stimulated, restless, and anxious, a quiet environment is needed rather than a high-traffic, bustling area (Option B). They require high-calorie, nutritious meals to keep up with their increased metabolic demands, not low-calorie diets (Option D). Fluid restriction (Option C) is inappropriate unless specifically indicated by a comorbidity, as they may lose fluids through diaphoresis.
</details>

---

### Question 210
A 19-year-old client is recovering in the critical care unit following a severe traumatic brain injury. The nurse empties 1,500 mL of very pale, dilute urine from the client's Foley catheter over just a two-hour period. Based on this observation, which additional clinical finding should the nurse immediately assess for?

A) Profuse diaphoresis  
B) Periorbital edema  
C) Signs of extreme dehydration  
D) Elevated capillary blood glucose  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
Massive polyuria with very dilute urine following a head injury is a hallmark sign of Diabetes Insipidus (DI), which occurs due to a decrease in antidiuretic hormone (ADH) production or release. This massive fluid loss rapidly leads to severe dehydration, hypernatremia, and hypovolemia. The nurse must immediately assess for signs of dehydration (poor skin turgor, dry mucous membranes, hypotension, tachycardia). Periorbital edema (Option B) indicates fluid overload, which is seen in SIADH, the opposite of DI. Elevated blood glucose (Option D) is characteristic of Diabetes Mellitus, not Diabetes Insipidus. Profuse diaphoresis (Option A) is not a specific finding related to DI.
</details>
### Question 211
A nurse in an outpatient clinic is reviewing medication instructions with a 45-year-old female recently prescribed levothyroxine for hypothyroidism. The patient states, "I will take this pill every morning with my scrambled eggs and coffee." Which action should the nurse take?

A. Advise the patient to take the medication at bedtime instead.  
B. Inform the patient to take the medication 30 to 60 minutes before breakfast.  
C. Document the patient's understanding of the medication regimen.  
D. Suggest taking the medication with a glass of milk to prevent stomach upset.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
Levothyroxine should be taken on an empty stomach, typically 30 to 60 minutes before breakfast, to ensure optimal absorption. Taking it with food (especially calcium-rich foods like milk or eggs) or coffee can significantly decrease its absorption, making the medication less effective. Therefore, the nurse must correct the patient's misunderstanding.
</details>

---

### Question 212
A 10-year-old boy with type 1 diabetes arrives at the school nurse's office complaining of feeling shaky, dizzy, and hungry. The nurse observes that his skin is pale and diaphoretic. Which is the best initial action by the nurse?

A. Encourage the child to lie down and rest for 15 minutes.  
B. Administer his scheduled dose of regular insulin.  
C. Provide 4 ounces of apple juice to the child immediately.  
D. Call the child's parents to take him home for the day.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
The child is exhibiting classic signs of hypoglycemia (shakiness, dizziness, hunger, pallor, and diaphoresis). The nurse's immediate action should be to administer a fast-acting, easily absorbable carbohydrate, such as 4 ounces of apple juice, to raise the child's blood glucose levels quickly. Giving insulin would worsen the hypoglycemia, and resting or calling parents delays necessary emergency treatment.
</details>

### Question 213
A nurse is caring for a 28-year-old female patient admitted to the medical-surgical unit with an exacerbation of Graves' disease. The patient is restless and reports feeling uncomfortably hot despite the room feeling normal to the staff. Which intervention should the nurse implement?

A. Provide extra blankets to prevent sudden chills.  
B. Encourage the patient to ambulate frequently in the hallway.  
C. Offer the patient hot tea or coffee to promote relaxation.  
D. Adjust the room thermostat to a cooler temperature.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br>
Graves' disease causes hyperthyroidism, leading to an increased metabolic rate. Patients often experience severe heat intolerance, diaphoresis, and restlessness. Providing a cool, calm environment by lowering the room temperature helps promote patient comfort. Giving hot beverages or extra blankets would exacerbate her discomfort, and frequent ambulation or caffeine (in coffee/tea) would further increase her already elevated metabolic rate and restlessness.
</details>

### Question 214
A nurse is monitoring a 22-year-old male patient in the intensive care unit following a traumatic brain injury. Over the last two hours, the nurse notes a sudden increase in urine output to 800 mL/hr of very pale, clear urine. The patient complains of extreme thirst. Which condition should the nurse suspect the patient is developing?

A. Diabetes insipidus  
B. Syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone (SIADH)  
C. Diabetic ketoacidosis  
D. Acute kidney injury  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
Diabetes insipidus (DI) is a common complication of traumatic brain injury or neurosurgery due to decreased production or release of antidiuretic hormone (ADH) from the pituitary gland. It is characterized by massive polyuria (large amounts of dilute urine) and polydipsia (excessive thirst). SIADH presents with decreased, concentrated urine output. Acute kidney injury typically presents with oliguria or anuria. Diabetic ketoacidosis would present with high blood glucose and ketones, not primarily triggered by head trauma in this manner.
</details>

---

### Question 215
A nurse is performing a routine skin and body assessment on a 68-year-old male resident in a long-term care facility who has a history of Cushing's syndrome. Which clinical observation should the nurse anticipate finding during the assessment?

A. Unexplained weight loss and profound muscle atrophy in the abdomen.  
B. Hyperpigmentation of the gums and oral mucosa.  
C. Purple striae on the abdomen and a rounded "moon" face.  
D. Thickened, dry, and scaly skin on the bilateral lower extremities.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
Cushing's syndrome is caused by an excess of corticosteroids (specifically glucocorticoids). Common physical findings observed by the nurse include central obesity, a rounded "moon" face, a fat pad on the back of the neck ("buffalo hump"), thin skin that bruises easily, and purplish-red striae (stretch marks) on the abdomen. Hyperpigmentation and weight loss are characteristic of Addison's disease, not Cushing's syndrome.
</details>
### Question 216
A 10-year-old boy with type 1 diabetes arrives at the school clinic pale, sweating, and reporting a headache. His capillary blood glucose is 58 mg/dL. Which action should the nurse take first?

A) Administer 4 ounces of regular fruit juice.
B) Give a scheduled dose of rapid-acting insulin.
C) Instruct the child to eat a high-protein energy bar.
D) Have the child rest quietly for 15 minutes before retesting.

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<b>Correct Answer: A</b><br>
The client is experiencing symptomatic hypoglycemia (blood glucose < 70 mg/dL). The nurse's first action should be to provide a fast-acting, easily absorbed simple carbohydrate, such as 4 ounces of regular fruit juice or regular soda, to quickly raise the blood glucose level. Giving insulin would worsen the hypoglycemia. A high-protein bar will not raise the blood sugar quickly enough for an acute episode. Resting and retesting without providing carbohydrates delays necessary treatment.
</details>
