Adequate systemic hydration is the most effective way to thin respiratory secretions, making them easier to expectorate. Unless the client has a contraindication (such as heart failure or severe kidney disease), encouraging 2 to 3 liters of fluid daily is highly recommended for pneumonia. Cough suppressants are generally contraindicated because the client needs to cough to clear the infection. Strict bed rest is not ideal, as mobility helps clear secretions, and limiting fluids would cause secretions to become thicker and harder to clear.
</details>

---

### Question 424
A nurse is caring for a 55-year-old client who is 12 hours postoperative following an abdominal hysterectomy. The client's oxygen saturation is 94% on room air, and the nurse auscultates diminished breath sounds in the lung bases. Which nursing action is most appropriate to prevent further respiratory complications?

A. Administer a prescribed PRN dose of intravenous morphine sulfate.  
B. Instruct the client to use an incentive spirometer every hour while awake.  
C. Prepare to assist the healthcare provider with an emergency intubation.  
D. Apply a non-rebreather mask at 15 L/min to increase oxygen saturation.  

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<b>Correct Answer: B</b><br>
Postoperative clients are at a high risk for atelectasis (collapse of alveoli), which manifests as diminished breath sounds in the lung bases. Instructing the client to use an incentive spirometer promotes deep breathing, maximizes lung expansion, and helps prevent atelectasis and subsequent pneumonia. An oxygen saturation of 94% is acceptable, so a non-rebreather mask or emergency intubation is entirely unwarranted. Morphine sulfate is an opioid that depresses the respiratory system and would not help expand the lungs, though pain control is necessary for the client to *be able* to take deep breaths.
</details>

---

### Question 425
A 2-year-old toddler is brought to the emergency department by parents who report the child has a "barking" cough and a hoarse cry. The nurse observes mild inspiratory stridor when the child is crying. Which initial action by the nurse is the most appropriate?

A. Use a tongue depressor to firmly inspect the back of the child's throat.  
B. Separate the child from the parents to perform a full physical assessment.  
C. Keep the child as calm as possible in the parents' arms.  
D. Place the child in a supine position on the examination table.  

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<b>Correct Answer: C</b><br>
The child is presenting with classic signs of croup (laryngotracheobronchitis). Agitation and crying significantly increase the work of breathing and worsen airway obstruction and stridor. The priority nursing action is to keep the child calm and comfortable, typically by allowing the child to remain in the parents' arms. Using a tongue depressor is strictly contraindicated in a child with suspected airway swelling (like epiglottitis or severe croup), as it can trigger a spasm and complete airway obstruction. Placing the child supine or separating them from the parents will increase anxiety and respiratory distress.
</details>
### Question 426
A 7-year-old client is brought to the emergency department experiencing an acute asthma exacerbation. The nurse administers an inhaled short-acting bronchodilator as prescribed. Which clinical observation best indicates to the nurse that the treatment was effective?

A) The child's oxygen saturation drops from 92% to 89% on room air.
B) The nurse notes newly diminished breath sounds at the lung bases.
C) The child exhibits a decrease in audible wheezing and decreased work of breathing.
D) The child becomes highly lethargic and stops crying.

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C

**Rationale:** A decrease in wheezing along with a decreased work of breathing indicates that the bronchodilator has effectively relaxed the smooth muscles of the airways, allowing for better airflow. Diminished breath sounds (Option B) and lethargy (Option D) are signs of impending respiratory failure in an asthmatic child. A drop in oxygen saturation (Option A) indicates worsening hypoxia, not improvement.
</details>

---

### Question 427
The nurse is caring for a 78-year-old client with chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) on a medical-surgical unit. The client reports becoming extremely exhausted and severely short of breath while eating meals. Which action should the nurse implement to best assist this client?

A) Provide frequent, small, high-protein meals throughout the day.
B) Administer a prescribed sedative 30 minutes before mealtime.
C) Encourage the client to drink a large glass of water while chewing food.
D) Deliver three large, heavy meals daily to maximize caloric intake.

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A

**Rationale:** Clients with COPD exert a significant amount of energy just to breathe. Eating large meals can place pressure on the diaphragm and cause severe fatigue. Providing frequent, small, high-calorie, and high-protein meals reduces the energy required to eat and prevents the stomach from over-expanding. Sedatives (Option B) can depress respiratory drive. Drinking large amounts of fluid with meals (Option C) can lead to early satiety and reduce nutritional intake. Large, heavy meals (Option D) will worsen the client's dyspnea and fatigue.
</details>

---

### Question 428
A 45-year-old client is seen in the outpatient clinic with a diagnosis of community-acquired pneumonia. The client complains of a frequent, thick, productive cough that is difficult to clear. Which recommendation should the nurse include in the discharge teaching?

A) Restrict fluid intake to less than 1 liter per day to dry up the respiratory secretions.
B) Maintain a flat, supine position while resting in bed to promote chest drainage.
C) Take an over-the-counter cough suppressant every 4 hours around the clock.
D) Increase oral fluid intake to 2 to 3 liters per day unless contraindicated.

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D

**Rationale:** Increasing oral fluid intake to 2 to 3 liters per day helps to thin respiratory secretions, making them easier to expectorate. Restricting fluids (Option A) will cause secretions to become thicker and harder to cough up. A supine position (Option B) reduces lung expansion and impairs the clearance of airway secretions; the client should sit up or use a semi-Fowler's position. Cough suppressants (Option C) are generally avoided during the day for a productive cough, as the client needs to expel the infected sputum from the lungs.
</details>

---

### Question 429
A 22-year-old client in the intensive care unit requires suctioning of their newly placed tracheostomy tube. The nurse assesses the client and gathers the necessary equipment. Which action should the nurse take immediately before inserting the suction catheter?

A) Instill 5 mL of sterile normal saline into the tracheostomy tube.
B) Hyperoxygenate the client with 100% oxygen using a manual resuscitation bag.
C) Apply continuous suction while advancing the catheter down the airway.
D) Ask the client to swallow forcefully to open the vocal cords.

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B

**Rationale:** Suctioning removes oxygen from the airway along with secretions, placing the client at risk for hypoxemia. The nurse must hyperoxygenate the client (usually with 100% oxygen) before and after each suction pass. Routine instillation of normal saline (Option A) is no longer recommended as it can wash bacteria into the lungs and cause oxygen desaturation. Suction should never be applied during insertion (Option C); it is only applied intermittently while withdrawing the catheter. Swallowing (Option D) does not affect a tracheostomy tube, which enters the trachea directly below the vocal cords.
</details>

---

### Question 430
A 15-year-old student comes to the school nurse's office with a sudden, spontaneous nosebleed (epistaxis). Which instruction should the nurse give to the student as the initial intervention?

A) "Lie flat on the cot and place an ice pack on the back of your neck."
B) "Tilt your head back as far as possible so the blood doesn't drip."
C) "Sit upright, lean your head slightly forward, and pinch the soft part of your nose."
D) "Blow your nose forcefully to remove any blood clots from your nasal airway."

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C

**Rationale:** The correct initial action for epistaxis is to have the client sit upright and lean forward, applying direct pressure by pinching the soft part of the nose for 5 to 15 minutes. Leaning forward prevents blood from draining down the throat, which can cause nausea and vomiting. Lying flat (Option A) or tilting the head back (Option B) increases the risk of swallowing blood or aspirating. Blowing the nose forcefully (Option D) will dislodge any clots that are forming and prolong the bleeding.
</details>
### Question 431
A nurse in an outpatient clinic is teaching the parents of an 8-year-old child recently diagnosed with asthma how to use a metered-dose inhaler (MDI) with a spacer. Which observation indicates that the teaching has been effective?

A) The child inhales as quickly and forcefully as possible through the mouthpiece.  
B) The parents instruct the child to exhale fully into the spacer before pressing the canister.  
C) The child takes a slow, deep breath in after the medication is released into the spacer.  
D) The parents remove the spacer because the child can hold the inhaler directly to the lips.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
When using an MDI with a spacer, taking a slow, deep breath ensures that the medication is drawn effectively into the lower airways rather than hitting the back of the throat. Inhaling too quickly (Option A) can cause the medication to deposit in the mouth or throat. Exhaling into the spacer (Option B) pushes the medication out of the device. The spacer should not be removed (Option D), especially for a pediatric patient, as it greatly improves medication delivery to the lungs.
</details>

---

### Question 432
A nurse on a medical-surgical unit needs to collect a sputum specimen for culture and sensitivity from a 65-year-old client admitted with bacterial pneumonia. Which action should the nurse take to ensure the most accurate sample?

A) Obtain the specimen first thing in the morning before the client eats or brushes their teeth.  
B) Have the client use an antibacterial mouthwash immediately before coughing up the sputum.  
C) Collect the clear saliva that the client spits into the cup after clearing their throat.  
D) Provide the client with a large meal to stimulate mucus production before the collection.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
Sputum specimens are best collected early in the morning because secretions pool overnight, providing a concentrated sample. Collecting it before eating or oral care prevents contamination from food particles or altering the normal flora. Using an antibacterial mouthwash (Option B) can kill the bacteria needed for the culture. Saliva (Option C) is not an acceptable specimen; the sample must come from deep within the lungs. Eating a large meal (Option D) does not stimulate mucus production and increases the risk of aspiration during coughing.
</details>

---

### Question 433
A home health nurse is observing a 72-year-old client with severe chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) perform pursed-lip breathing during a walk around the living room. Which action by the client requires the nurse to intervene?

A) The client relaxes their neck and shoulder muscles while breathing.  
B) The client breathes in slowly through their nose for a count of two.  
C) The client puckers their lips as if they are about to whistle while exhaling.  
D) The client exhales forcefully through pursed lips for a count of one.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br>
This is an intervention question, meaning the correct answer is the action the client is doing *incorrectly*. During pursed-lip breathing, exhalation should be slow, gentle, and unforced, lasting about twice as long as inhalation (e.g., in for 2 seconds, out for 4 seconds). Exhaling forcefully for a short duration (Option D) defeats the purpose of the exercise, which is to keep the airways open longer and prevent air trapping. Options A, B, and C are all correct steps for pursed-lip breathing.
</details>

---

### Question 434
A nurse is caring for a 45-year-old client who is recovering from abdominal surgery. To prevent respiratory complications such as atelectasis, the nurse instructs the client to use an incentive spirometer. Which statement by the nurse represents the correct instruction?

A) "Blow into the mouthpiece as hard as you can to raise the piston."  
B) "Inhale slowly and deeply through the mouthpiece to raise the indicator."  
C) "Pump the mouthpiece with short, rapid breaths to clear your airways."  
D) "Breathe in through your nose and out through the spirometer tube."  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
The purpose of an incentive spirometer is to encourage maximal sustained inhalation, which mimics sighing or yawning, to expand the alveoli and prevent atelectasis. Therefore, the client should be instructed to inhale slowly and deeply through the mouthpiece (Option B). Blowing into the spirometer (Option A), taking rapid breaths (Option C), or breathing out through the tube (Option D) are incorrect uses of the device.
</details>

---

### Question 435
A 25-year-old client arrives at the emergency department with shortness of breath and an oxygen saturation of 89% on room air. The healthcare provider orders oxygen at 2 L/min via nasal cannula. After applying the device, what is a priority maintenance action for the nurse?

A) Check the skin behind the client's ears and inside the nares for signs of irritation.  
B) Apply petroleum jelly to the client's nares to prevent the mucosa from drying out.  
C) Ensure the oxygen flow meter is turned up to 6 L/min to quickly boost oxygen levels.  
D) Instruct the client to breathe entirely through their mouth to maximize oxygen intake.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
Oxygen tubing can cause friction and pressure, leading to skin breakdown behind the ears, on the cheeks, and inside the nares. Assessing skin integrity is a priority nursing action for a client on a nasal cannula. Petroleum jelly (Option B) is combustible and should never be used with oxygen therapy; a water-soluble lubricant should be used instead. The nurse cannot increase the oxygen flow beyond the ordered amount without a provider's prescription (Option C). The client should breathe through their nose, not exclusively their mouth (Option D), to receive the oxygen delivered via the nasal cannula.
</details>
### Question 436
A nurse is admitting a 6-year-old child with a history of frequent generalized tonic-clonic seizures to the pediatric unit. Which action is the highest priority for the nurse to implement during the admission process?

A) Placing a padded tongue blade at the bedside.
B) Dimming the lights in the child's room permanently.
C) Ensuring working suction equipment and oxygen are at the bedside.
D) Assigning the child to a room at the far end of the hallway.

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) Ensuring working suction equipment and oxygen are at the bedside.

**Rationale:** The priority for a client with a history of seizures is maintaining a patent airway and ensuring safety. Having working suction and oxygen readily available allows the nurse to immediately clear the airway and provide oxygenation during or after a seizure. Placing a tongue blade in the mouth during a seizure is strictly contraindicated as it can cause airway obstruction or dental trauma. The child should be near the nurse's station, not at the end of the hallway, for close observation. 
</details>

---

### Question 437
A nurse in the emergency department is caring for a 45-year-old client who sustained a mild concussion after a fall. While performing a routine neurological check, the nurse notes the client, who was previously awake and talking, is now extremely difficult to arouse and mumbles incomprehensibly. What is the nurse's immediate next action?

A) Notify the healthcare provider of the change in condition.
B) Document the findings as a normal post-concussive sleep pattern.
C) Administer the prescribed oral pain medication for the client's headache.
D) Elevate the client's legs to improve blood flow to the brain.

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) Notify the healthcare provider of the change in condition.

**Rationale:** A sudden change or decrease in the level of consciousness (LOC) is the earliest and most sensitive indicator of increasing intracranial pressure (ICP) or neurological decline. The nurse must immediately notify the healthcare provider so prompt interventions can be initiated. Documenting it as normal delays care, and elevating the legs could potentially increase intracranial pressure further. 
</details>

---

### Question 438
A nurse in a rehabilitation center is assisting a 72-year-old client with their lunch. The client recently had an ischemic stroke resulting in right-sided facial weakness and dysphagia. Which technique should the nurse use to promote safe swallowing?

A) Offer thin liquids using a standard drinking straw.
B) Position the client in a semi-Fowler's position at 30 degrees.
C) Mix solid foods and liquids together in the same bite.
D) Place the food on the left, unaffected side of the client's mouth.

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) Place the food on the left, unaffected side of the client's mouth.

**Rationale:** Placing food on the unaffected (left) side of the mouth helps the client control the food bolus, chew effectively, and reduces the risk of pocketing and aspiration. Clients with dysphagia should sit upright at 90 degrees (high-Fowler's), avoid straws as they can speed up liquid delivery and cause choking, and avoid mixing different consistencies of food in a single bite.
</details>

---

### Question 439
A 22-year-old client has just returned to the observation area following a lumbar puncture to rule out meningitis. Which intervention should the nurse implement first to prevent post-procedure complications?

A) Ambulate the client to the bathroom to empty their bladder.
B) Instruct the client to remain flat in bed for several hours.
C) Restrict the client's oral fluid intake for the rest of the day.
D) Apply a warm compress over the lumbar puncture site.

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) Instruct the client to remain flat in bed for several hours.

**Rationale:** Following a lumbar puncture, the client should remain flat in bed for a prescribed amount of time (usually 4 to 8 hours) to prevent cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) leakage and the development of a severe spinal headache. Ambulating the client immediately increases the risk of a spinal headache. Fluid intake should be encouraged, not restricted, to help replace the withdrawn CSF. 
</details>

---

### Question 440
A home health nurse is observing a 68-year-old client with advanced Parkinson's disease walking from the living room to the kitchen. The nurse notes the client has a freezing, shuffling gait and is at risk for falling. Which instruction should the nurse provide to help the client walk more safely?

A) "Imagine stepping over a line on the floor with each step."
B) "Look down at your feet constantly while you are moving."
C) "Slide your feet across the floor rather than lifting them."
D) "Try to lean forward as much as possible when you walk."

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) "Imagine stepping over a line on the floor with each step."

**Rationale:** Clients with Parkinson's disease often experience a shuffling gait or "freezing" episodes. Instructing the client to consciously lift their feet and imagine stepping over a line or an object helps bypass the basal ganglia defect, prompting a more normal stride and reducing the risk of tripping. Looking at the feet or leaning forward alters their center of gravity and increases the risk of falls.
</details>
### Question 441
A nurse is caring for an 8-year-old child in the pediatric unit. While sitting in a bedside chair playing a game, the child suddenly loses consciousness and begins to have a tonic-clonic seizure. What is the nurse's immediate priority action?

A) Insert a padded tongue blade into the child's mouth to protect the airway.
B) Firmly restrain the child's arms and legs to prevent musculoskeletal injury.
C) Gently assist the child to the floor and place a soft pad under their head.
D) Leave the room quickly to retrieve the emergency crash cart and suction equipment.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
During an active seizure, the primary nursing goal is safety and injury prevention. If a client is in a chair or standing, the nurse should immediately ease them to the floor and protect their head with a pad or pillow. Restraining the client (Option B) can cause fractures or muscle tears. Nothing should ever be inserted into the mouth of a person having a seizure (Option A), as it can cause dental damage or obstruct the airway. The nurse should never leave a actively seizing client alone (Option D); instead, they should call for help while staying with the child.
</details>

---

### Question 442
A nurse is receiving a 75-year-old client from the emergency department who recently sustained an ischemic stroke. The nursing handoff notes that the client has dysphagia. Which action should the nurse implement during mealtime to promote safety?

A) Provide a straw to help the client consume all thin liquids.
B) Instruct the client to tuck their chin downward when swallowing.
C) Place the client in a 30-degree semi-Fowler's position during the meal.
D) Encourage the client to eat quickly before they become fatigued.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
Dysphagia (difficulty swallowing) is common after a stroke and places the client at a high risk for aspiration. Tucking the chin downward toward the chest when swallowing narrows the airway, helping to prevent food and liquid from entering the trachea. Using a straw (Option A) is contraindicated as it propels liquids to the back of the throat too quickly, increasing aspiration risk. The client should be positioned upright at 90 degrees (high-Fowler's), not 30 degrees (Option C). The client should be encouraged to eat slowly and be given ample time to chew and swallow to prevent choking (Option D).
</details>

---

### Question 443
A nurse is observing a 60-year-old client with Parkinson's disease walk down the hallway in a long-term care facility. Based on the client's diagnosis, which characteristic gait pattern should the nurse anticipate observing?

A) A high-stepping gait with a noticeable foot drop on one side.
B) A wide-based, unsteady, and staggering walk.
C) A scissors-like gait where the knees frequently cross each other.
D) A posture leaning forward with short, shuffling steps.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br>
Parkinson's disease is a neurodegenerative disorder affecting motor function. A hallmark clinical manifestation is a propulsive or shuffling gait, characterized by a stooped, forward-leaning posture and short, quick, shuffling steps. A high-stepping gait with foot drop (Option A) is typically associated with lower motor neuron disease or peroneal nerve injury. An unsteady, staggering, wide-based gait (Option B) is indicative of cerebellar ataxia. A scissors gait (Option C) is commonly seen in clients with cerebral palsy or multiple sclerosis due to spasticity. 
</details>

---

### Question 444
A 20-year-old college athlete is evaluated in the student health center after sustaining a mild concussion during a soccer game. The nurse is providing discharge instructions to the client's roommate. Which statement by the roommate indicates a need for further teaching by the nurse?

A) "I will give him an aspirin right away if he complains of a bad headache."
B) "I will bring him back to the clinic immediately if he starts vomiting repeatedly."
C) "I will make sure he stays away from bright computer screens and loud music today."
D) "I will ask him his name and where he is when I check on him to ensure he is alert."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
This is a "need for further teaching" (negative-style) question. Aspirin and other NSAIDs (like ibuprofen) increase the risk of bleeding and are contraindicated immediately following a head injury due to the risk of intracranial hemorrhage. Acetaminophen is the preferred mild analgesic. The other statements show correct understanding: repeated vomiting is a sign of increasing intracranial pressure and requires immediate evaluation (Option B); cognitive and sensory rest, including avoiding bright screens, aids in concussion recovery (Option C); and assessing orientation ensures the client's level of consciousness is not deteriorating (Option D).
</details>

---

### Question 445
A 9-month-old infant is admitted to the pediatric intensive care unit with a sudden onset of fever, nuchal rigidity, and suspected bacterial meningitis. Which infection control action is the priority for the nurse to implement upon the infant's arrival?

A) Place the infant in a private room with negative airflow pressure.
B) Implement droplet precautions and ensure staff wear a surgical mask.
C) Initiate airborne precautions and require all visitors to wear an N95 respirator.
D) Use standard precautions and wear double gloves during diaper changes.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
Bacterial meningitis (such as that caused by *Neisseria meningitidis* or *Haemophilus influenzae*) is highly contagious and transmitted via large respiratory droplets. The priority action is to immediately initiate droplet precautions, which includes placing the client in a private room and having healthcare workers wear a surgical mask when within 3 feet of the client. Negative airflow rooms and N95 respirators (Options A and C) are required for airborne precautions (e.g., tuberculosis, measles), not droplet precautions. Standard precautions alone (Option D) are insufficient for suspected bacterial meningitis.
</details>
### Question 446
A nurse is admitting a 12-year-old patient to the pediatric unit who has a known history of generalized tonic-clonic seizures. Which action should the nurse take first when preparing the patient's room to ensure safety?

A) Place a padded tongue blade on the bedside table.
B) Dim the overhead lights to promote a restful environment.
C) Apply protective padding to the side rails of the bed.
D) Request a prescription for a soft, easy-to-chew diet.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C) Apply protective padding to the side rails of the bed.</strong><br><br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> For a patient with a history of seizures, initiating seizure precautions is the top priority to prevent injury. This includes padding the side rails of the bed so the patient does not strike their head or limbs against hard surfaces during a seizure. Placing a tongue blade (padded or otherwise) in a patient's mouth during a seizure is contraindicated as it can cause airway obstruction or dental trauma. Dimming lights and modifying the diet do not directly address the immediate physical safety needs related to seizure activity.
</details>

---

### Question 447
A triage nurse in the emergency department is evaluating a 68-year-old patient whose spouse reports that the patient suddenly began slurring their words. Which action should the nurse perform next to quickly assess for a suspected acute stroke?

A) Ask the patient to smile and observe for facial symmetry.
B) Palpate the patient's carotid pulses simultaneously.
C) Auscultate the patient's apical heart rate for one full minute.
D) Check the patient's deep tendon reflexes in the lower extremities.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A) Ask the patient to smile and observe for facial symmetry.</strong><br><br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Sudden slurred speech is a cardinal sign of an acute ischemic or hemorrhagic stroke. The nurse should immediately perform a rapid neurological assessment, such as the FAST (Face, Arms, Speech, Time) screening. Asking the patient to smile allows the nurse to observe for facial droop or asymmetry. Palpating carotid pulses simultaneously is dangerous as it can compromise cerebral blood flow. Auscultating the apical pulse and checking deep tendon reflexes, while part of a comprehensive exam, are not the immediate priority assessments for a suspected acute stroke.
</details>

---

### Question 448
A nurse is conducting a home health visit for a 78-year-old patient recently diagnosed with Parkinson's disease. The nurse observes the patient walking with a forward posture and a shuffling gait. Which intervention is the priority for the nurse to implement?

A) Instruct the patient to look directly at their feet while walking.
B) Encourage the patient to walk as fast as possible to build momentum.
C) Advise the patient to wear smooth socks without shoes inside the house.
D) Remove loose throw rugs and clutter from the patient's walking paths.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D) Remove loose throw rugs and clutter from the patient's walking paths.</strong><br><br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Patients with Parkinson's disease often experience a shuffling, propulsive gait, which places them at a high risk for falls. The priority nursing intervention is to modify the environment for safety, which includes removing tripping hazards like loose throw rugs and clutter. Patients should be taught to look up, not at their feet, to maintain better balance. Walking fast increases the risk of falls due to an inability to stop suddenly. Wearing smooth socks without shoes increases the risk of slipping and is unsafe.
</details>

---

### Question 449
A nurse on a medical-surgical unit is assigned to care for a 22-year-old college student admitted with suspected bacterial meningitis. Which infection control action must the nurse take before entering the patient's room?

A) Put on a sterile gown and sterile gloves.
B) Don a standard surgical mask.
C) Put on an N95 respirator mask.
D) Apply a face shield and a disposable hairnet.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B) Don a standard surgical mask.</strong><br><br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Bacterial meningitis is highly contagious and is transmitted through large respiratory droplets. Therefore, the nurse must initiate droplet precautions, which require wearing a standard surgical mask when entering the patient's room or coming within 3 feet of the patient. Sterile gowns and gloves are not required for standard infection control isolation. An N95 respirator is required for airborne precautions (e.g., tuberculosis), not droplet precautions. A face shield may be used if splashing is anticipated, but the surgical mask is the primary, required barrier.
</details>

---

### Question 450
A nurse is assessing a 45-year-old patient who is under overnight observation in a step-down unit following a minor concussion. The nurse needs to evaluate the patient's orientation as part of the routine neurological assessment. Which question is most appropriate for the nurse to ask?

A) "Do you remember the name of the nurse who admitted you last night?"
B) "Can you count backward from 100 by 7s for me?"
C) "Can you tell me your full name and where you are right now?"
D) "What did you have to eat for breakfast this morning?"

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C) "Can you tell me your full name and where you are right now?"</strong><br><br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Assessing orientation involves verifying the patient's awareness of person, place, and time. Asking the patient to state their full name and current location directly assesses orientation to person and place. Asking about the admitting nurse or breakfast assesses short-term memory, not orientation. Counting backward by 7s (serial sevens) assesses attention, concentration, and cognitive function, but does not assess baseline orientation.
</details>
### Question 451
An 8-year-old child is admitted to the pediatric unit for observation after experiencing a generalized tonic-clonic seizure at school. As the nurse prepares the child's room prior to their arrival, which action is the priority?

A) Place padded tongue blades on the bedside table.
B) Ensure suction equipment and oxygen are set up at the bedside.
C) Request a prescription for soft wrist restraints from the provider.
D) Keep the bed in the highest position for easy physical assessments.

<details>
<summary><strong>Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) Ensure suction equipment and oxygen are set up at the bedside.

**Rationale:** The priority for a patient with a history of seizures is maintaining a patent airway and ensuring safety. Suction equipment and oxygen must be readily available at the bedside to manage secretions and provide respiratory support if another seizure occurs. Padded tongue blades are contraindicated as inserting objects into the mouth during a seizure can cause oral injury or airway obstruction. The bed should be kept in the lowest position, and restraints should not be used during a seizure.
</details>

---

### Question 452
A 35-year-old client on a medical-surgical unit has just returned to their room following a lumbar puncture to rule out meningitis. Which intervention should the nurse implement immediately upon the client's return?

A) Instruct the client to remain flat in bed for the prescribed amount of time.
B) Encourage the client to ambulate in the hallway to prevent deep vein thrombosis.
C) Elevate the head of the bed to 45 degrees to promote lung expansion.
D) Restrict oral fluids for the next 12 hours to prevent nausea.

<details>
<summary><strong>Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) Instruct the client to remain flat in bed for the prescribed amount of time.

**Rationale:** After a lumbar puncture, the client is typically instructed to lie flat (supine) for several hours to prevent a cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) leak, which can cause a severe spinal headache. Ambulating or elevating the head of the bed too soon increases the risk of a spinal headache. Unless contraindicated, oral fluids should actually be encouraged, not restricted, to help replace the withdrawn CSF.
</details>

---

### Question 453
A 72-year-old client is recovering on a rehabilitation unit following an ischemic stroke. The client has right-sided weakness and dysphagia. When assisting the client with lunch, which action should the nurse take to prevent aspiration?

A) Provide a straw for all liquids to make swallowing easier.
B) Position the client in a semi-Fowler's position at a 30-degree angle.
C) Instruct the client to tuck their chin down toward their chest while swallowing.
D) Place the food directly onto the weaker side of the client's mouth.

<details>
<summary><strong>Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) Instruct the client to tuck their chin down toward their chest while swallowing.

**Rationale:** The "chin-tuck" method helps protect the airway and prevents aspiration by narrowing the tracheal opening and widening the esophageal opening. Using a straw can deliver liquids too quickly to the back of the throat, increasing aspiration risk. The client should sit upright at a 90-degree angle (high-Fowler's), not a 30-degree angle, during meals. Food should be placed on the unaffected (stronger) side of the mouth to ensure proper chewing and swallowing.
</details>

---

### Question 454
A 19-year-old college student is brought to the Emergency Department after sustaining a mild concussion during a soccer game. The nurse is performing frequent neurological checks. Which observation requires the nurse to notify the healthcare provider immediately?

A) The client complains of a mild, dull, continuous headache.
B) The client asks for an extra blanket and a glass of water.
C) The client has a small, superficial abrasion on their forehead.
D) The client becomes unusually drowsy and is difficult to wake up.

<details>
<summary><strong>Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) The client becomes unusually drowsy and is difficult to wake up.

**Rationale:** A decrease or change in the level of consciousness (such as extreme drowsiness or difficulty rousing) is often the earliest sign of increased intracranial pressure (ICP) or neurological decline, requiring immediate medical intervention. A mild headache and minor abrasions are expected findings following a concussion and can be monitored routinely. Asking for a blanket or water indicates the client is alert and oriented to their needs.
</details>

---

### Question 455
A 65-year-old client with advanced Parkinson's disease is walking to the examination room in an outpatient clinic. Suddenly, the client stops moving and states that their feet feel "glued to the floor." Which action should the nurse take to assist the client?

A) Tell the client to imagine stepping over a line on the floor.
B) Pull the client gently by the arm to initiate forward movement.
C) Instruct the client to look up at the ceiling and take a quick, large step.
D) Provide a wheelchair immediately to transport them the rest of the way.

<details>
<summary><strong>Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) Tell the client to imagine stepping over a line on the floor.

**Rationale:** Clients with Parkinson's disease often experience "freezing" of gait. Using visual or cognitive cues, such as imagining a line to step over, dropping a tissue to step over, or counting out loud, can help the client overcome the freezing episode and safely initiate movement. Pulling the client alters their center of gravity and increases the risk of a fall. Looking up at the ceiling also impairs balance. While a wheelchair could transport them, promoting independence and using mobility strategies is the appropriate first nursing intervention.
</details>
### Question 456

A nurse is providing discharge instructions to the family of a 19-year-old client recently diagnosed with a seizure disorder. Which statement by the family member indicates a correct understanding of how to safely manage a seizure at home?

A) "I will place a soft washcloth in his mouth to protect his tongue from being bitten."
B) "I will hold his arms and legs firmly to prevent him from hurting his joints."
C) "I will immediately give him a glass of water to drink as soon as the shaking stops."
D) "I will move nearby furniture away and place a pillow under his head."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Correct Answer</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D

**Rationale:** During a seizure, the primary goal is to protect the client from injury. Moving furniture away and placing a soft object, like a pillow, under the head prevents traumatic injury. Nothing should ever be placed in the client's mouth (Option A) due to the risk of airway obstruction or breaking the client's teeth. Restraining the client (Option B) can cause musculoskeletal injuries. Offering liquids immediately after a seizure (Option C) places the client at high risk for aspiration due to a decreased level of consciousness during the postictal phase.
</details>

---

### Question 457

A 72-year-old client is admitted to the medical unit following an acute ischemic stroke. The unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP) prepares to assist the client with their first meal since admission. Which action by the nurse is essential before allowing the client to eat?

A) Ensure the client is lying completely flat in bed for comfort.
B) Provide the client with a straw to make drinking liquids easier.
C) Check the client's chart for the results of a swallowing evaluation.
D) Ask the UAP to cut all the client's food into small, bite-sized pieces.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Correct Answer</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C

**Rationale:** Clients who have suffered a stroke are at a high risk for dysphagia (difficulty swallowing) and subsequent aspiration. It is critical for the nurse to verify that a swallowing evaluation has been completed and passed before the client is given any food, fluids, or oral medications. The client should be positioned upright, not flat (Option A). Straws (Option B) often increase the risk of aspiration by depositing liquids too quickly into the back of the throat. While cutting food into small pieces (Option D) is helpful, checking the swallow study takes priority.
</details>

---

### Question 458

A 28-year-old client has just returned to the medical-surgical unit after undergoing a lumbar puncture to rule out meningitis. The nurse is assisting the client into bed. Which action is most appropriate for the nurse to implement regarding the client's post-procedure care?

A) Instruct the client to remain flat in bed for several hours as prescribed.
B) Elevate the head of the client's bed to 45 degrees to ease breathing.
C) Restrict the client's fluid intake for the next 12 hours.
D) Encourage the client to ambulate the hallways immediately to prevent blood clots.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Correct Answer</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A

**Rationale:** Following a lumbar puncture, clients are typically instructed to remain flat in bed (supine or prone) for several hours to prevent a cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) leak from the puncture site, which can cause a severe "spinal headache." Elevating the head (Option B) or early ambulation (Option D) increases the risk of a spinal headache. Fluid intake should actually be encouraged, not restricted (Option C), to help replenish CSF.
</details>

---

### Question 459

A 50-year-old client is admitted to the neurosurgical intensive care unit following a severe traumatic brain injury. The nurse is positioning the client in bed to help manage increased intracranial pressure (ICP). Which action by the nurse is correct?

A) Flexing the client's neck forward with two thick pillows.
B) Elevating the head of the bed to 30 degrees with the head in a neutral position.
C) Placing the client in a prone position to promote drainage of oral secretions.
D) Keeping the bed completely flat with the client's knees sharply flexed.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Correct Answer</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B

**Rationale:** To prevent elevations in intracranial pressure (ICP) and promote optimal venous drainage from the brain, the nurse should elevate the head of the bed to 15-30 degrees and maintain the head and neck in a neutral, midline position. Neck flexion (Option A) and sharp hip/knee flexion (Option D) obstruct venous return and increase intra-abdominal pressure, which in turn increases ICP. The prone position (Option C) is contraindicated for a client with increased ICP.
</details>

---

### Question 460

A nurse in a long-term care facility is observing a 79-year-old client with Parkinson's disease ambulate to the dining room. The client suddenly experiences "freezing" of their gait and states they cannot step forward. What is the most appropriate action for the nurse to take?

A) Gently push the client forward from the shoulders to create momentum.
B) Tell the client to close their eyes, relax, and try again in five minutes.
C) Tell the client to imagine stepping over a specific line on the floor.
D) Immediately provide a wheelchair and instruct the client not to walk alone anymore.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Correct Answer</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C

**Rationale:** Clients with Parkinson's disease often experience "freezing" episodes where they feel their feet are glued to the floor. The nurse can help the client overcome this by providing visual or auditory cues, such as imagining stepping over a line on the floor, marching in place, or stepping over the nurse's foot. Pushing the client (Option A) increases the risk for a fall. Closing their eyes (Option B) removes visual input and impairs balance. A wheelchair (Option D) reduces the client's independence and mobility, which should be maintained as long as safely possible.
</details>
### Question 461
A nurse on a medical-surgical unit is admitting a 24-year-old client with a known history of generalized tonic-clonic seizures. Which action is the priority for the nurse to take when preparing the client's room?

A) Place a padded tongue blade at the bedside table for easy access.
B) Ensure suction equipment and oxygen are set up and functioning properly.
C) Request a prescription from the provider for soft wrist restraints.
D) Keep the room brightly lit at all times to accurately monitor the client.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
When setting up a room for a client with a history of seizures, the nurse's priority is airway and safety. Having working suction equipment and oxygen at the bedside ensures that the nurse can quickly clear the airway of secretions and provide oxygen during or immediately after a seizure. Padded tongue blades are contraindicated as inserting anything into the mouth during a seizure can cause severe injury. Restraints should never be used during a seizure, and keeping the room brightly lit can disrupt sleep, which is a common seizure trigger.
</details>

---

### Question 462
An emergency department nurse is evaluating a 68-year-old client who sustained a minor head injury from a fall at home. While assessing the client's level of consciousness using the Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS), the nurse applies a mild painful stimulus by squeezing the trapezius muscle. The client immediately opens their eyes in response. How should the nurse document this specific finding?

A) Spontaneous eye opening
B) Eye opening to speech
C) Eye opening to pain
D) No eye opening

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
The Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS) assesses eye-opening, verbal, and motor responses. Because the client only opened their eyes after the nurse applied a painful stimulus (trapezius squeeze), this is documented as "eye opening to pain," which scores a 2 in the eye-opening category. Spontaneous opening would mean the eyes were already open without any stimulation. 
</details>

---

### Question 463
A nurse in an outpatient neurology clinic is caring for a 45-year-old client who just completed a lumbar puncture to rule out meningitis. Which instruction should the nurse provide to the client immediately following the procedure?

A) "You need to lie flat on your back for the next few hours."
B) "You should sit in a high-Fowler's position to promote drainage."
C) "You must walk around the clinic for 15 minutes to prevent blood clots."
D) "You will need to restrict your fluid intake for the rest of the day."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
Following a lumbar puncture, clients are typically instructed to lie flat (supine) for several hours. This prevents the leakage of cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) from the puncture site, which helps reduce the risk of a severe post-lumbar puncture headache (spinal headache). Fluids should actually be encouraged, not restricted, to help replenish CSF volume. Sitting upright or walking immediately after the procedure increases the risk of a spinal headache.
</details>

---

### Question 464
A nurse in a rehabilitation facility is preparing to assist an 82-year-old client with right-sided weakness and dysphagia following an ischemic stroke. Which action should the nurse take to ensure the client's safety while assisting them with their lunch?

A) Provide large bites of food to encourage independent chewing.
B) Place the client in a semi-Fowler's position at a 30-degree angle.
C) Instruct the client to tilt their head backward when swallowing the food.
D) Position the client upright at a 90-degree angle in a chair or bed.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br>
Positioning the client completely upright at a 90-degree angle (high-Fowler's or in a chair) is the most critical intervention to prevent aspiration in a client with dysphagia. The head should be tucked slightly forward (chin-to-chest) rather than tilted backward, as tilting the head back opens the airway and increases aspiration risk. Bites of food should be small, not large. 
</details>

---

### Question 465
A nurse at a long-term care facility is walking down the hallway with a 75-year-old client who has Parkinson's disease. The client suddenly stops, appears frozen, and states, "My feet feel like they are glued to the floor." Which action should the nurse take to assist the client?

A) Gently pull the client forward by their arms to initiate movement.
B) Instruct the client to look up at the ceiling and take a deep breath.
C) Tell the client to imagine a line on the floor and step over it.
D) Bring a wheelchair immediately and tell the client they can no longer walk.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
"Freezing" of gait is a classic, temporary symptom of Parkinson's disease where the client feels stuck to the floor. Using visual or auditory cues, such as asking the client to step over an imaginary line or a laser pointer, helps bypass the basal ganglia and triggers the brain to initiate voluntary movement. Pulling the client can cause a fall due to their postural instability. Giving up on ambulation and getting a wheelchair prematurely decreases the client's independence.
</details>
### Question 466
A nurse is admitting an 8-year-old child with a history of generalized tonic-clonic seizures to the pediatric unit. Which action is most important for the nurse to take when preparing the child's room?

A. Place a padded tongue blade on the bedside table.  
B. Ensure functioning suction equipment is at the bedside.  
C. Keep the bed in the highest position for easy access.  
D. Dim the lights completely to promote rest.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
When placing a client on seizure precautions, the nurse must ensure that oxygen and functioning suction equipment are readily available at the bedside to help maintain a clear airway if the client vomits or produces excess secretions during or after a seizure. Placing anything in the client's mouth, such as a tongue blade, is contraindicated due to the risk of choking and dental damage. The bed should be kept in the lowest position to prevent falls, and while a calm environment is helpful, completely dimming the lights is not a priority safety measure.
</details>

---

### Question 467
A triage nurse in the emergency department evaluates a 65-year-old client brought in by their spouse. The spouse reports that the client suddenly developed slurred speech and right-sided facial drooping. Which action should the nurse take first?

A. Determine the exact time the symptoms started.  
B. Draw blood to check the client's lipid profile.  
C. Request a prescription for a statin medication.  
D. Ask the client to drink a small cup of water.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
The client is exhibiting classic signs of a stroke. The priority action for the nurse is to establish the "last known well" time (the exact time symptoms started), as this determines the client's eligibility for thrombolytic therapy (such as tPA), which is highly time-sensitive. Checking a lipid profile or administering statins are not immediate emergency priorities. Asking the client to drink water is unsafe, as the client may have dysphagia from the stroke and is at high risk for aspiration.
</details>

---

### Question 468
A home health nurse is visiting a 72-year-old client diagnosed with Parkinson's disease. The nurse observes the client exhibiting a shuffling gait and leaning forward while walking. Which instruction should the nurse provide to the client to decrease the risk of falling?

A. "Look directly at your feet while you are walking."  
B. "Keep your arms tight against your sides when you walk."  
C. "Consciously lift your toes when stepping."  
D. "Walk as quickly as you can to maintain your momentum."  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
Clients with Parkinson's disease often develop a shuffling gait, which increases their risk for tripping and falling. The nurse should instruct the client to consciously lift their toes, step deliberately, and swing their arms to maintain balance. Looking at the feet can shift the center of gravity forward and worsen the forward-leaning posture, increasing fall risk. Keeping arms tight against the sides hinders balance. Walking quickly can lead to a festinating gait, where the client involuntarily speeds up, further increasing the risk of a fall.
</details>

---

### Question 469
A 35-year-old client has just returned to the medical-surgical unit after undergoing a lumbar puncture to rule out meningitis. Which nursing intervention is appropriate for this client's immediate post-procedure care?

A. Encourage the client to sit upright in a chair.  
B. Restrict oral fluid intake for the next 4 hours.  
C. Ambulate the client in the hallway to prevent deep vein thrombosis.  
D. Instruct the client to lie flat in bed for several hours.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br>
Following a lumbar puncture, the client is typically instructed to remain flat in bed for several hours (usually 4 to 8 hours depending on facility protocol). This helps prevent a post-lumbar puncture headache, which can be caused by the leakage of cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) from the puncture site. Sitting upright or ambulating immediately after the procedure increases the likelihood of a severe headache. Oral fluids should be encouraged, not restricted, to help replace the lost CSF. 
</details>

---

### Question 470
A nurse on the neurological step-down unit is caring for a 22-year-old client who sustained a concussion during a sports injury. The nurse performs hourly neurological checks. Which observation should the nurse identify as the earliest indicator of increased intracranial pressure (ICP)?

A. The client's pupils become fixed and dilated.  
B. The client's heart rate drops to 45 beats per minute.  
C. The client becomes unusually restless and confused.  
D. The client exhibits decorticate posturing.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
A change in the level of consciousness (LOC)—such as new-onset restlessness, confusion, agitation, or lethargy—is the earliest and most sensitive indicator of increased intracranial pressure (ICP). Fixed and dilated pupils, bradycardia (part of Cushing's triad), and abnormal posturing (decorticate or decerebrate) are late and ominous signs of increased ICP and brain herniation.
</details>
### Question 471
A 22-year-old patient with a history of generalized tonic-clonic seizures is admitted to the medical-surgical unit for monitoring. While setting up the patient's room, which action should the nurse take *first* to ensure the patient's safety?

A) Pad the side rails of the patient's bed.
B) Restrict oral fluid intake to prevent aspiration.
C) Place a padded tongue blade at the bedside.
D) Keep the patient securely restrained in bed at all times.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A) Pad the side rails of the patient's bed.</strong><br>
Padding the side rails is a fundamental component of seizure precautions to prevent physical injury if the patient experiences a seizure while in bed. Restricting oral fluid intake is unnecessary and could lead to dehydration. Placing a tongue blade (padded or otherwise) in a patient's mouth during a seizure is strictly contraindicated as it can cause severe injury to the teeth and airway. Restraining a patient during a seizure or as a preventative measure can cause severe musculoskeletal trauma and is contraindicated.
</details>

---

### Question 472
A 65-year-old patient has just returned to the neurology step-down unit after undergoing a diagnostic lumbar puncture. The nurse is providing instructions to the patient regarding post-procedure care. Which statement by the nurse is the most appropriate?

A) "You should sit in a high-Fowler's position to relieve pressure."
B) "You need to limit your fluid intake for the next 12 hours."
C) "You will need to lie flat on your back for several hours."
D) "You should ambulate in the hallway immediately to prevent blood clots."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C) "You will need to lie flat on your back for several hours."</strong><br>
Following a lumbar puncture, the patient is typically instructed to lie flat (supine) for several hours. This minimizes the risk of cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) leakage from the puncture site and helps prevent the development of a severe spinal headache. Sitting up or ambulating immediately increases the risk of a spinal headache. Patients are usually encouraged to increase, not limit, fluid intake to help replenish CSF.
</details>

---

### Question 473
A 78-year-old client in a rehabilitation unit is recovering from a right-sided stroke (CVA) and has been diagnosed with left-sided weakness and mild dysphagia. When assisting the client with lunch, which action by the nurse is the most appropriate to prevent aspiration?

A) Mix thin liquids with solid foods to make chewing easier.
B) Encourage the client to tilt their head back when swallowing.
C) Keep the head of the bed elevated at 30 degrees during the meal.
D) Place the food on the unaffected side of the client's mouth.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D) Place the food on the unaffected side of the client's mouth.</strong><br>
Placing food on the unaffected (stronger) side of the mouth allows the client to chew and control the food bolus more effectively, significantly reducing the risk of choking and aspiration. Mixing thin liquids with solids can actually increase the risk of aspiration. Tilting the head back opens the airway and increases aspiration risk; a chin-tuck position is preferred. The head of the bed should be elevated to 90 degrees (high-Fowler's) during meals, not 30 degrees.
</details>

---

### Question 474
A 45-year-old patient is brought to the emergency department after a minor motor vehicle collision. While conducting a baseline neurological assessment, the nurse asks the patient to state their full name, their current location, and the current year. What is the nurse primarily assessing with these questions?

A) Cranial nerve function
B) Orientation to person, place, and time
C) Short-term memory recall
D) Receptive aphasia

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B) Orientation to person, place, and time</strong><br>
Asking a patient for their name, location, and the date/year is the standard clinical method for assessing a patient's orientation to person, place, and time (often documented as "Alert and Oriented x3"). Cranial nerve function involves specific sensory and motor tests (e.g., smiling, tracking a penlight). Short-term memory is typically assessed by asking the patient to recall a set of random words after a few minutes. Receptive aphasia is assessed by evaluating the patient's ability to understand and follow spoken commands.
</details>

---

### Question 475
A 16-year-old client is admitted to the pediatric intensive care unit following a sports-related concussion. During a neurological check, the nurse shines a penlight into the client's right eye and observes the left pupil constricting simultaneously. How should the nurse document this specific clinical finding?

A) Consensual pupillary response
B) Direct pupillary response
C) Accommodation
D) Nystagmus

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A) Consensual pupillary response</strong><br>
A consensual pupillary response occurs when the pupil of one eye constricts in response to light being shone into the opposite eye. This is a normal and expected neurological finding. A direct pupillary response refers to the constriction of the pupil in the eye that the light is directly shone into. Accommodation is the adjustment of the eyes (constriction and convergence) when focusing on a near object after looking at a distant one. Nystagmus is an involuntary, rhythmic twitching or movement of the eyes.
</details>
### Question 476
A nurse is admitting a 25-year-old client with a history of generalized tonic-clonic seizures to the medical-surgical unit. The nurse is delegating the room setup to the assistive personnel (AP). Which action by the AP requires the nurse to intervene immediately?

A. Placing the client's bed in the lowest possible position.
B. Securing padded tongue blades at the head of the bed.
C. Setting up oxygen and suction equipment at the bedside.
D. Placing soft padding along the side rails of the bed.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> B

<strong>Rationale:</strong> Inserting any object, including padded tongue blades, into the mouth of a client actively experiencing a seizure is contraindicated. It can cause severe dental trauma or airway obstruction. The nurse must intervene and remove the tongue blades. Placing the bed in the lowest position, setting up oxygen and suction, and padding the side rails are all appropriate standard seizure precautions.
</details>

---

### Question 477
A nurse in a rehabilitation facility is assisting a 72-year-old client who recently had a right-sided ischemic stroke. The client currently exhibits left-sided facial weakness and mild dysphagia. What is the most appropriate action by the nurse during mealtime?

A. Instruct the client to tilt their head backward when swallowing.
B. Offer thin liquids to clear the client's throat between bites.
C. Place the food on the right side of the client's mouth.
D. Encourage the client to eat quickly before fatigue sets in.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> C

<strong>Rationale:</strong> For clients with unilateral facial weakness and dysphagia following a stroke, food should be placed on the unaffected (stronger) side of the mouth—in this case, the right side. This facilitates proper chewing and reduces the risk of aspiration. The head should be tucked forward (chin-to-chest), not tilted back, to protect the airway. Thin liquids often increase aspiration risk, and meals should be unhurried to ensure safe swallowing.
</details>

---

### Question 478
An outpatient clinic nurse is observing a 68-year-old client with Parkinson's disease walk down the hallway to the examination room. The client suddenly stops and reports feeling like their feet are "glued to the floor." Which instruction should the nurse provide to assist the client?

A. "Try to imagine stepping over a line on the floor."
B. "Lean forward and use your momentum to push off."
C. "Look directly down at your feet while you walk."
D. "Pivot quickly on your heels to break the freeze."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> A

<strong>Rationale:</strong> "Freezing of gait" is a common manifestation of Parkinson's disease. Using visual or auditory cues, such as imagining a line on the floor to step over, helps bypass the basal ganglia block and allows the client to initiate movement. Leaning forward or pivoting quickly significantly increases the risk of a fall. Looking directly down at the feet promotes poor posture and can impair balance.
</details>

---

### Question 479
A 35-year-old client has just returned to the emergency department observation room after undergoing a diagnostic lumbar puncture. Which intervention should the nurse implement immediately following the procedure?

A. Ambulate the client to the bathroom to void.
B. Encourage the client to cough and deep breathe vigorously.
C. Elevate the head of the bed to a high-Fowler's position.
D. Instruct the client to remain flat in bed for the prescribed time.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> D

<strong>Rationale:</strong> Following a lumbar puncture, the client is typically instructed to remain flat in bed (supine) for a prescribed period, usually 4 to 8 hours depending on facility protocol. This minimizes the risk of cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) leakage from the puncture site and prevents a severe post-lumbar puncture headache. Early ambulation, sitting upright, and vigorous coughing can alter CSF pressure and increase the risk of complications.
</details>

---

### Question 480
A nurse is caring for a 19-year-old client admitted to the intensive care unit with a severe traumatic brain injury. The client is at risk for increased intracranial pressure (ICP). Which nursing action is most appropriate to help prevent an increase in the client's ICP?

A. Keep the head of the bed elevated at 30 degrees.
B. Perform nasotracheal suctioning routinely every hour.
C. Cluster all nursing care activities together to provide long rest periods.
D. Flex the client's neck to maintain an open and clear airway.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> A

<strong>Rationale:</strong> Elevating the head of the bed to 30 degrees promotes venous drainage from the brain via the jugular veins, which helps prevent an increase in intracranial pressure (ICP). Suctioning increases ICP and should only be performed when clinically necessary, never on a routine schedule. Clustering care can cause a sustained, dangerous spike in ICP, so nursing activities should be spaced out. The client's head and neck should be kept in a neutral, midline position; flexing the neck obstructs venous return and increases ICP.
</details>
### Question 481
A nurse is assisting an 82-year-old client with right-sided weakness following a stroke to eat lunch in an inpatient rehabilitation facility. Which action by the nurse promotes client safety during the meal?

A) Elevate the head of the bed to 30 degrees.
B) Encourage the client to use a straw for liquids.
C) Place the food on the left side of the client's mouth.
D) Mix solid foods and liquids in the same bite.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: C**

**Rationale:** Placing food on the unaffected (left) side of the mouth helps prevent aspiration and pocketing of food in clients with unilateral weakness. The head of the bed should be elevated to at least 90 degrees (high-Fowler's), straws are often contraindicated as they can speed up liquid intake and cause choking, and mixing textures can confuse the swallowing mechanism.
</details>

---

### Question 482
A 22-year-old client with a history of epilepsy suddenly loses consciousness and begins to have tonic-clonic movements while resting in a medical-surgical bed. What is the nurse's immediate priority action?

A) Turn the client to a side-lying position.
B) Insert a padded tongue blade into the client's mouth.
C) Restrain the client's limbs firmly to prevent joint dislocation.
D) Administer an oral antiepileptic medication immediately.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: A**

**Rationale:** The priority during a seizure is to maintain a patent airway and ensure safety. Turning the client to the side allows saliva and oral secretions to drain out of the mouth, preventing aspiration. The nurse should never insert anything into the mouth or physically restrain a seizing client, as this can cause severe injury. Oral medications are a choking hazard during an active seizure.
</details>

---

### Question 483
A nurse is caring for a 45-year-old client in an outpatient clinic who has just returned to the observation room following a lumbar puncture. Which instruction should the nurse provide to the client?

A) "You should sit upright in a chair for the next 4 hours."
B) "You need to limit your fluid intake for the rest of the day."
C) "You should perform coughing exercises every 15 minutes."
D) "You need to lie flat in bed for the prescribed amount of time."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: D**

**Rationale:** Lying flat for a specified period (usually 4 to 8 hours) after a lumbar puncture helps prevent a cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) leak from the puncture site and significantly reduces the risk of a severe post-lumbar puncture headache. Fluids should be encouraged, not limited, to help replenish CSF. Sitting upright or coughing increases intracranial and spinal pressure, worsening the risk of a leak.
</details>

---

### Question 484
A home health nurse is visiting a 70-year-old client with Parkinson's disease. While walking toward the kitchen, the nurse observes the client's feet suddenly "freezing" in place when attempting to step through the doorway. Which action should the nurse take to assist the client?

A) Pull the client quickly by the arm through the doorway.
B) Tell the client to imagine stepping over a line on the floor.
C) Instruct the client to look up at the ceiling while walking.
D) Have the client pivot rapidly on one foot to break the freeze.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: B**

**Rationale:** "Freezing" of gait is a common motor symptom in Parkinson's disease. Having the client imagine stepping over a line, or placing an actual object like a laser pointer line on the floor, helps bypass the impaired basal ganglia pathways and provides a visual cue to initiate movement. Pulling the client or pivoting rapidly increases the risk of falls. Looking at the ceiling alters balance and is unsafe.
</details>

---

### Question 485
A 16-year-old client is brought to the emergency department after hitting their head in a skateboarding accident. During the neurological assessment, the nurse notes that the client's pupils are unequal in size and sluggish to react to light. What is the nurse's best action?

A) Notify the healthcare provider immediately.
B) Dim the lights in the room and recheck the pupils in one hour.
C) Ask the client to read a visual acuity chart on the wall.
D) Administer a prescribed oral analgesic for the headache.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: A**

**Rationale:** Unequal and sluggish pupils following a head injury are a classic, ominous sign of increased intracranial pressure (ICP) and potential brain herniation. This is a medical emergency that requires immediate notification of the healthcare provider. Waiting to recheck, performing routine visual acuity tests, or giving oral pain medications delays critical, life-saving interventions.
</details>
### Question 486
A nurse is admitting an 8-year-old child to the pediatric floor with a history of frequent tonic-clonic seizures. Which action should the nurse take when preparing the child's room?

A) Place a padded tongue blade on the bedside table.
B) Ensure the bed is in the lowest position and pad the side rails. 
C) Keep the room brightly lit to closely monitor the child from the doorway.
D) Assign the child to a room at the end of the hallway for maximum privacy.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B) Ensure the bed is in the lowest position and pad the side rails.</strong><br><br>
Standard seizure precautions require maintaining a safe environment to prevent injury if a seizure occurs. This includes keeping the bed in its lowest position and padding the side rails. A padded tongue blade (Option A) should never be placed in a patient's mouth during a seizure, as it can cause injury or airway obstruction. Bright lights (Option C) can sometimes trigger seizures and prevent rest. Patients with a high risk for seizures should be placed close to the nurses' station for quick observation and intervention, not at the end of the hall (Option D).
</details>

---

### Question 487
A 45-year-old patient returns to the medical-surgical unit after undergoing a lumbar puncture to rule out meningitis. Which instruction should the nurse provide to the patient regarding post-procedure care?

A) "You should sit in a high-Fowler's position to prevent a severe headache."
B) "Limit your fluid intake for the next 12 hours to prevent fluid overload."
C) "Ambulate in the hallway immediately to prevent blood clots in your legs."
D) "Remain lying flat in bed for several hours as prescribed."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D) "Remain lying flat in bed for several hours as prescribed."</strong><br><br>
Following a lumbar puncture, patients are typically instructed to remain lying flat (supine) for several hours. This prevents cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) leakage from the puncture site and decreases the risk of a post-lumbar puncture headache. Sitting up (Option A) or ambulating immediately (Option C) increases the risk of a spinal headache. Patients are actually encouraged to increase fluid intake, not limit it (Option B), to help replenish CSF.
</details>

---

### Question 488
A nurse is assisting an 82-year-old resident with right-sided hemiplegia and mild dysphagia during mealtime at a long-term care facility. Which action is most appropriate for the nurse to take to prevent aspiration?

A) Instruct the resident to tuck their chin to their chest when swallowing.
B) Provide all beverages through a straw to make swallowing easier.
C) Place each bite of food carefully into the right side of the resident's mouth.
D) Keep the head of the bed elevated at 30 degrees during the meal.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A) Instruct the resident to tuck their chin to their chest when swallowing.</strong><br><br>
Tucking the chin towards the chest while swallowing helps close off the trachea, significantly reducing the risk of aspiration in patients with dysphagia. Using a straw (Option B) is contraindicated for patients with dysphagia because it deposits liquid quickly into the back of the throat, increasing aspiration risk. Food should be placed on the unaffected side of the mouth—which is the left side for this patient, not the right (Option C). The head of the bed should be elevated to 90 degrees (high-Fowler's), not 30 degrees (Option D), during meals.
</details>

---

### Question 489
A 20-year-old patient is brought to the emergency department after sustaining a minor head injury during a soccer game. The nurse assesses the patient by asking them to state their name, where they are, and the current year. The patient answers all questions accurately. Which specific neurological parameter is the nurse assessing with this action?

A) Motor response to verbal commands.
B) Cranial nerve function and pupillary reaction.
C) Level of consciousness and orientation.
D) Sensory perception and reflex pathways.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C) Level of consciousness and orientation.</strong><br><br>
Asking a patient for their name, location, and the date assesses their level of consciousness and orientation to person, place, and time (often documented as "Alert and Oriented x3" or "x4"). This is a basic and essential component of the Glasgow Coma Scale (verbal response) and general neurological assessment. This action does not directly assess motor responses (Option A), cranial nerves/pupils (Option B), or sensory/reflex pathways (Option D).
</details>

---

### Question 490
A nurse is caring for a 75-year-old patient with advanced Alzheimer's disease in a memory care unit. The nurse observes the patient pacing rapidly back and forth in a crowded hallway, looking highly agitated and wringing their hands. What is the nurse's best initial action?

A) Apply soft wrist restraints to prevent the patient from harming others.
B) Gently redirect the patient to a quiet, calm environment.
C) Administer a PRN dose of an anti-anxiety medication immediately.
D) Ask the patient to explain exactly why they are feeling so upset.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B) Gently redirect the patient to a quiet, calm environment.</strong><br><br>
Patients with advanced Alzheimer's disease can easily become overstimulated, leading to agitation and pacing. The best initial, non-pharmacological nursing action is to reduce environmental stimuli by gently guiding the patient to a quiet, calm area. Physical restraints (Option A) and chemical restraints (Option C) should be avoided unless the patient is an immediate danger to themselves or others, and only after conservative measures fail. Asking a patient with advanced dementia "why" they are upset (Option D) is ineffective and can increase agitation, as they often lack the cognitive ability to process or articulate the reason.
</details>
### Question 491
A nurse is preparing a hospital room for the admission of an 8-year-old child with a history of new-onset generalized seizures. Which is the priority nursing action to ensure a safe environment for this client?

A) Place a padded tongue blade on the bedside table.
B) Ensure the side rails of the bed are padded.
C) Verify that four-point restraints are under the bed.
D) Keep the room brightly lit to observe the child easily.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) Ensure the side rails of the bed are padded.

**Rationale:** Padding the side rails is a standard, fundamental seizure precaution to prevent physical injury if a seizure occurs while the child is in bed. Tongue blades should never be placed in a client's mouth during a seizure due to the risk of breaking teeth or obstructing the airway.
